Differential graded Koszul duality: an introductory survey
Leonid Positselski

Abstract
. This is an overview on derived nonhomogeneous Koszul duality over a field, mostly based on the author’s memoir [ 64 ] . The paper is intended to serve as a pedagogical introduction and a summary of the covariant duality between DG-algebras and curved DG-coalgebras, as well as the triality between DG-modules, CDG-comodules, and CDG-contramodules. Some personal reminiscences are included as a part of historical discussion.



Introduction

Koszul duality is a fundamental phenomenon in mathematics, including
such fields as algebraic topology, algebraic and differential geometry,
and representation theory.
This phenomenon is so general that it does not seem to admit
a “maximal natural” generality.
Whatever formulation one comes up with, it can be likely further
extended by building something on top or underneath.

Koszul duality is a synthetic subject.
Depending on context, its formulation involves such concepts as
differential graded structures, curved diffferential graded structures,
comodules and contramodules, conilpotency, coderived and contraderived
categories, operads and properads, and whatnot.

Koszul duality has to be distinguished from
the
comodule-contramodule correspondence
.
The co-contra correspondence is a less familiar though no less
fundamental, but simpler phenomenon, often accompanying
the Koszul duality.
The following rule of thumb for distinguishing the two phenomena
may be helpful.
Viewed as a covariant category equivalence, the co-contra
correspondence takes injective objects to projective objects or
vice versa.
Koszul duality takes projective objects to irreducible objects and/or
irreducible objects to injective objects etc.
[
75
, Prologue]
.

The aim of this paper is to introduce the reader to the subject of
derived Koszul duality in the context of differential graded algebras
and modules, as well as coalgebras, comodules, and contramodules;
and survey some of its main results.
We also discuss curved differential structures, but
no

\mathrm{A}_{\infty}
-structures are considered in this paper
(the reader can find discussions of these in the Koszul duality
context in the dissertation
[
47
]
and
the memoirs
[
64
,
65
]
).

No
relative Koszul duality settings (as in
[
63
, Sections 0.4
and 11]
or
[
75
]
) are discussed in this survey, either;
so everything in this paper happens over a field.
We also do not consider operads and their generalizations, referring
instead to the book
[
48
]
and the papers
[
31
,
89
]
.

This introductory survey is intended for an audience versed in
homological algebra generally, but largely unfamiliar with the subject
of derived Koszul duality.
It is almost entirely based on the author’s memoir
[
64
]
.
The more elementary topic of
underived
quadratic
duality
[
83
,
61
,
7
,
58
]
is only briefly touched in this paper.

Homogeneous
versions of derived Koszul duality
[
7
]
,

[
64
, Appendix A]
(involing graded modules over positively
or negatively graded algebras, with the differentials preserving
the grading) are not elaborated upon in this paper, either.
Model structures and compact generators are also only briefly touched.

We start with posing the problem in a basic particular case,
formulating the main results in an approximate form in the simplest
settings, and then proceed to introduce further ingredients and make
more precise and general assertions.
Some sketches of proofs are included in this survey, but most
results receive a brief outline of an idea of the proof and
a reference to a more detailed treatment.

All the Koszul duality functors, triangulated equivalences etc. in
this paper are
covariant
functors or category equivalences.
A discussion of
contravariant
version of
homogeneous

Koszul duality can be found in
[
64
, Section A.2]
.

One disclaimer is in order.
As usual in differential graded homological algebra, sign rules
(plus or minus) present a tedious problem.
In this survey, we skip many descriptions of sign rules, referring
the reader to the original publications such as
[
64
]

for the details.
So our formulations may be imprecise in that not all the signs
are properly spelled out.

1.
Algebras and Modules

Throughout this paper, we work over a fixed ground field
k
.
Unless otherwise mentioned, all
algebras
in this paper are
associative and unital, all
modules
are unital, and all

homomorphisms of algebras
are presumed to take the unit to
the unit.

In this section we start posing the problem of derived nonhomogeneous
Koszul duality in the simplest particular case of complexes of modules
over an augmented
k
-algebra
A
.

1.1.
Augmented algebras

Let
A
be an (associative, unital) algebra over a field
k
.
An
augmentation

\alpha
on
A
is a (unital)
k
-algebra
homomorphism
\alpha\colon A\longrightarrow k
.
So
\alpha(1)=1
, and the existence of an augmentation implies that

1\neq 0
in
A
; hence
\alpha
is a surjective map.
We denote by
A^{+}=\ker(\alpha)\subset A
the augmentation ideal;
so
A^{+}
is a two-sided ideal in
A
, and
A=k\oplus A^{+}
as
a
k
-vector space.
The
k
-algebra homomorphism
\alpha\colon A\longrightarrow k
endows
the one-dimensional
k
-vector space
k
with left and right

A
-module structures.

The following definition goes back to
the papers
[
20
, Chapter II]
and
[
1
]
.
The
bar-construction

\Br^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\alpha}(A)
of an augmented

k
-algebra
A=(A,\alpha)
is defined as the complex

k\overset{0}{\mskip 1.5mu\leftarrow\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\relbar\mskip 1.5mu}A^{+}\overset{\partial}{\mskip 1.5mu\leftarrow\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\relbar\mskip 1.5mu}A^{+}\otimes_{k}A^{+}\overset{\partial}{\mskip 1.5mu\leftarrow\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\relbar\mskip 1.5mu}A^{+}\otimes_{k}A^{+}\otimes_{k}A^{+}\mskip 1.5mu\leftarrow\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\relbar\mskip 1.5mu\dotsb

with the diffential given by the formulas
\partial(a\otimes b)=ab
,
\partial(a\otimes b\otimes c)=ab\otimes c-a\otimes bc
, …,

\partial(a_{1}\otimes\dotsb\otimes a_{n})=a_{1}a_{2}\otimes a_{3}\otimes\dotsb\otimes a_{n}-\dotsb\\
+(-1)^{i+1}a_{1}\otimes\dotsb\otimes a_{i-1}\otimes a_{i}a_{i+1}\otimes a_{i+2}\otimes\dotsb\otimes a_{n}+\dotsb\\
+(-1)^{n}a_{1}\otimes\dotsb\otimes a_{n-2}\otimes a_{n-1}a_{n},

etc., for all
a
,
b
,
c
,
a_{i}\in A^{+}
,
n\geq 1
, and

1\leq i\leq n-1
.
The leftmost differential
\partial\colon A^{+}\longrightarrow k
is the zero map.

The complex
\Br^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\alpha}(A)
computes the
k
-vector spaces

\Tor^{A}_{*}(k,k)
(where
k
is endowed with left and right

A
-module structures via
\alpha
).
In other words, there are natural isomorphisms of
k
-vector
spaces

H^{-n}\Br^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\alpha}(A)\simeq\Tor^{A}_{n}(k,k)\qquad\text{for all $n\geq 0$}.

Let
(A,\alpha)
and
(B,\beta)
be two augmented
k
-algebras,
and let
f\colon A\longrightarrow B
be a
k
-algebra homomorphism compatible
with the augumentations (i. e., satisfying the equation
\alpha=\beta f
).
Then there is the induced map of augmentation ideals
f^{+}\colon A^{+}\longrightarrow B^{+}
and the induced map of the bar-constructions

\Br^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(f)\colon\Br^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\alpha}(A)\longrightarrow\Br^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\beta}(B)
.
The latter map is a morphism of complexes of
k
-vector spaces.

Example 1.1
.

The morphism of complexes
\Br^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(f)
may well be a quasi-isomorphism
even when a morphism of augmented
k
-algebras
f\colon A\longrightarrow B

is
not
an isomorphism.

For example, the complex
0\longleftarrow B^{+}\longleftarrow B^{+}\otimes_{k}B^{+}\longleftarrow B^{+}\otimes_{k}B^{+}\otimes_{k}B^{+}\longleftarrow\dotsb
is acyclic whenever
the augmentation ideal
B^{+}
, viewed as an associative
k
-algebra,
has a unit of its own.
The sequence of
k
-linear maps
h\colon B^{+}{}^{\otimes n}\longrightarrow B^{+}{}^{\otimes n+1}
given by the rules
h(b_{1}\otimes\dotsb\otimes b_{n})=e\otimes b_{1}\otimes\dotsb\otimes b_{n}
for all
b_{i}\in B^{+}
,
1\leq i\leq n
,

n\geq 1
, where
e
is the unit in
B^{+}
, provides
a contracting homotopy.

Choose any nonzero associative, unital
k
-algebra, denote it by

B^{+}
, and adjoin a new unit to it formally, producing
the
k
-algebra
B=k\oplus B^{+}
.
Then the natural homomorphisms of augmented
k
-algebras
k\longrightarrow B\longrightarrow k
induce quasi-isomorphisms of the bar-constructions.

More generally, let
A
and
B
be two associative, unital

k
-algebras.
Consider the direct sum
A\oplus B
, and endow it with a
k
-algebra
structure as the product of
A
and
B
in the category of

k
-algebras.
Choose an augmentation
\alpha\colon A\longrightarrow k
, and let the augmentation
of
A\oplus B
be constructed as the composition
A\oplus B\longrightarrow A\overset{\alpha}{\longrightarrow}k
.
Then the natural homomorphism of augmented
k
-algebras
A\oplus B\longrightarrow A
(the direct summand projection) induces a quasi-isomorphism
of the bar-constructions.

1.2.
Bar-constructions of modules

Let
A
be an associative
k
-algebra with an augmentation

\alpha\colon A\longrightarrow k
.
Let
M
be a left
A
-module.

The
bar-construction

\Br^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\alpha}(A,M)
of
A
with
the coefficients in
M
is the complex

M\overset{\partial}{\mskip 1.5mu\leftarrow\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\relbar\mskip 1.5mu}A^{+}\otimes_{k}M\overset{\partial}{\mskip 1.5mu\leftarrow\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\relbar\mskip 1.5mu}A^{+}\otimes_{k}A^{+}\otimes_{k}M\mskip 1.5mu\leftarrow\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\relbar\mskip 1.5mu\dotsb

with the diffferential given by the formulas
\partial(a\otimes m)=am
,
\partial(a\otimes b\otimes m)=ab\otimes\penalty m-a\otimes bm
, …,

\partial(a_{1}\otimes\dotsb\otimes a_{n}\otimes m)=a_{1}a_{2}\otimes a_{3}\otimes\dotsb\otimes a_{n}\otimes m-\dotsb+(-1)^{n+1}a_{1}\otimes\dotsb\otimes a_{n-1}\otimes a_{n}m,

etc., for all
a
,
b
,
a_{i}\in A^{+}
,
m\in M
, and
n\geq 1
.

The complex
\Br^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\alpha}(A,M)
computes the
k
-vector spaces

\Tor^{A}_{*}(k,M)
(where
k
is endowed with a right
A
-module
structure via
\alpha
).
In other words, there are natural isomorphisms of
k
-vector
spaces

H^{-n}\Br^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\alpha}(A,M)\simeq\Tor^{A}_{n}(k,M)\qquad\text{for all $n\geq 0$}.

Examples 1.2
.

The complex
\Br^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\alpha}(A,M)
may well be acyclic even when
M\neq 0
.

(1) For example, the complex
\Br^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\alpha}(A,M)
is acyclic whenever
the augmentation ideal
A^{+}
, viewed as an associative
k
-algebra,
has a unit of its own.
The sequence of
k
-linear maps
h\colon A^{+}{}^{\otimes n}\otimes_{k}M\longrightarrow A^{+}{}^{\otimes n+1}\otimes_{k}M
given by the rules
h(a_{1}\otimes\dotsb\otimes a_{n}\otimes m)=e\otimes a_{1}\otimes\dotsb\otimes a_{n}\otimes m
for all
a_{i}\in A^{+}
,
m\in
M,
1\leq i\leq n
,
n\geq 0
, where
e
is the unit in
A^{+}
, provides
a contracting homotopy.

More generally, let
A
and
B
be two associative, unital

k
-algebras, and let
\alpha\colon A\longrightarrow k
be an augmentation
of
A
.
Consider the direct sum
A\oplus B
, and endow it with an augmented

k
-algebra structure
(\alpha,0)\colon A\oplus B\longrightarrow k
as in
Example
1.1
.
Pick a left
B
-module
M
, and endow it with an
(A\oplus B)
-module
structure via the natural
k
-algebra homomorphism (the direct
summand projection)
A\oplus B\longrightarrow B
.
Then the complex
\Br^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{(\alpha,0)}(A\oplus B,\>M)
is acyclic.

(2) To give a couple of other examples, consider the algebra of
polynomials in one variable
A=k[x]
over the field
k
, endowed with
the augmentation
\alpha\colon A\longrightarrow k
given by the rule
\alpha(x)=0
.
For any element
a\in k
, denote by
k_{a}=A/(x-a)
the one-dimensional

A
-module in which the generator
x\in A
acts by the operator
of multiplication with
a
.
In particular, in the
A
-module
k=k_{0}
the algebra
A
acts via
the augmentation
\alpha
.
Put
M=k_{a}
, where
a\neq 0
.
Then
\Tor^{A}_{n}(k,M)=0
for all
n\geq 0
, hence the complex

\Br^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\alpha}(A,M)
is acyclic.

Alternatively, consider the
A
-module
M=k[x,x^{-1}]
of
Laurent polynomials in
x
(or the
A
-module
M=k(x)
of rational
functions in
x
).
Once again, in these cases
\Tor^{A}_{n}(k,M)=0
for all
n\geq 0
,
and the complex
\Br^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\alpha}(A,M)
is acyclic.

1.3.
Posing the problem

Let
A=(A,\alpha)
be an augmented
k
-algebra, and let
M^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
be
a complex of left
A
-modules.
Then the bar-construction
\Br^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\alpha}(A,M^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})
is a bicomplex of

k
-vector spaces.
Let us totalize this bicomplex by taking infinite direct sums along
the diagonals.

The problem of derived (nonhomogeneous) Koszul duality can be
formulated as follows.
We would like to endow the complex
\Br^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\alpha}(A,M^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})
with
some natural structure, and define an equivalence relation on complexes
with such structures, in a suitable way so that the assignment

M^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\longmapsto\Br^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\alpha}(A,M^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})

would be a triangulated equivalence between the unbounded derived
category
\mathsf{D}(A{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}})
of complexes of
A
-modules and the triangulated
category of complexes with the said structure up to the said
equivalence relation.

What structure should it be, and what should be the equivalence
relation?
The first question is easier to answer: the bar-construction

\Br^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\alpha}(A)
has a natural
DG-coalgebra
structure,
and the complexes
\Br^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(A,M^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})
are
DG-comodules
over

\Br^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\alpha}(A)
, as will be explained below in
Sections
2.3
–
2.4
.

The second question is harder, because the conventional notion of
quasi-isomorphism is
not
up to the task, as
Examples
1.2
illustrate.
The complex
\Br^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\alpha}(A,M)
can be quasi-isomorphic to zero
for a quite nonzero one-term complex of
A
-modules
M=M^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
.
The relevant definition of the
coderived category
of
DG-comodules will be spelled out in
Section
7.7
.

2.
Coalgebras and Comodules

Unless otherwise mentioned, all
coalgebras
in this paper are
coassociative, counital coalgebras over the field
k
, all

comodules
are counital, and all
homomorphisms of
coalgebras
are compatible with the counits.

In this section we present the simplest initial formulations of
derived nonhomogeneous Koszul duality for complexes of modules over
an augmented algebra
A
and for complexes of comodules over
a conilpotent coalgebra
C
.

2.1.
Coalgebras and comodules

The standard reference sources on coalgebras and comodules over
a field are the books
[
88
,
49
]
.
The present author’s overview
[
71
]
can be used as an additional
source.

A (
coassociative, counital
)
coalgebra

C
over
a field
k
is a
k
-vector space endowed with
k
-linear maps of

comultiplication
and
counit

\mu\colon C\longrightarrow C\otimes_{k}C\qquad\text{and}\qquad\epsilon\colon C\longrightarrow k

satisfying the following
coassociativity
and
counitality

axioms.
Firstly, the two compositions

C\longrightarrow C\otimes_{k}C\rightrightarrows C\otimes_{k}C\otimes_{k}C

must be equal to each other,
(\mu\otimes\mathrm{id}_{C})\circ\mu=(\mathrm{id}_{C}\otimes\mu)\circ\mu
.
Secondly, both the compositions

C\longrightarrow C\otimes_{k}C\rightrightarrows C

must be equal to the identity map,
(\epsilon\otimes\mathrm{id}_{C})\circ\mu=\mathrm{id}_{C}=(\mathrm{id}_{C}\otimes\epsilon)\circ\mu
.

A
left comodule

M
over a coalgebra
C
is a
k
-vector space
endowed with a
k
-linear
left coaction
map

\nu\colon M\longrightarrow C\otimes_{k}M

satisfying the following coassociativity and counitality axioms.
Firstly, the two compositions

M\longrightarrow C\otimes_{k}M\rightrightarrows C\otimes_{k}C\otimes_{k}M

must be equal to each other,
(\mu\otimes\mathrm{id}_{M})\circ\nu=(\mathrm{id}_{C}\otimes\nu)\circ\nu
.
Secondly, the composition

M\longrightarrow C\otimes_{k}M\longrightarrow M

must be equal to the identity map,
(\epsilon\otimes\mathrm{id}_{M})\circ\nu=\mathrm{id}_{M}
.

A
right comodule

N
over
C
is a
k
-vector space endowed
with a
right coaction
map

\nu\colon N\longrightarrow N\otimes_{k}C

satisfying the similar coassociativity and counitality axioms.

Let
V
be a
k
-vector space.
Then the comultiplication map
\mu
on a coalgebra
C
induces a left
coaction map
C\otimes_{k}V\longrightarrow C\otimes_{k}C\otimes_{k}V
on the
k
-vector space

C\otimes_{k}V
and a right coaction map
V\otimes_{k}C\longrightarrow V\otimes_{k}C\otimes_{k}C

on the
k
-vector space
V\otimes_{k}C
.
The left
C
-comodule
C\otimes_{k}V
and the right
C
-comodule
V\otimes_{k}C

are called the
cofree
C
-comodules
cogenerated by the vector
space
V
.

For any left
C
-comodule
L
, the
k
-vector space of all left

C
-comodule maps
L\longrightarrow C\otimes_{k}V
is naturally isomorphic to
the
k
-vector space of all
k
-linear maps
L\longrightarrow V
,

\Hom_{C}(L,\>C\otimes_{k}V)\simeq\Hom_{k}(L,V).

Hence the “cofree comodule” terminology.

2.2.
DG-coalgebras and DG-comodules

The following definitions, going back at least to the paper
[
23
]
,
can be also found in
[
64
, Sections 2.1 and 2.3]
.

A
graded coalgebra
over
k
is a graded
k
-vector space

C=\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}C^{i}
endowed with a coalgebra structure
such that both the comultiplication map
\mu\colon C\longrightarrow C\otimes_{k}C
and
the counit map
\epsilon\colon C\longrightarrow k
are morphisms of graded vector
spaces (i. e., homogeneous linear maps of degree
0
).
Here the standard induced grading is presumed on the tensor product

C\otimes_{k}C
, while the one-dimensional
k
-vector space
k
is
endowed with the grading where it is placed in the degree
i=0
.

A
graded left comodule
over a graded coalgebra
C
is
a graded
k
-vector space
M=\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}M^{i}
endowed with
a left
C
-comodule structure such that the coaction map

\nu\colon M\longrightarrow C\otimes_{k}M
is a morphism of graded vector spaces.

Graded right
C
-comodules
are defined similarly.

In particular, for any graded
k
-vector space
V
, the graded

k
-vector space
C\otimes_{k}V
has a natural structure of a cofree graded
left
C
-comodule, while the tensor product
V\otimes_{k}C
is a cofree
graded right
C
-comodule.

A
DG-coalgebra

C^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}=(C,d)
over
k
is a complex of

k
-vector spaces endowed with a coalgebra structure such that both
the comultiplication map
\mu\colon C^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\longrightarrow C^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\otimes_{k}C^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
and
the counit map
\epsilon\colon C^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\longrightarrow k
are morphisms of complexes
of vector spaces (i. e., homogeneous linear maps of degree
0

commuting with the differentials).
Here the standard induced differential
d(c^{\prime}\otimes c^{\prime\prime})=d(c^{\prime})\otimes c^{\prime\prime}+(-1)^{i}c^{\prime}\otimes d(c^{\prime\prime})
for all
c^{\prime}\in C^{i}
and
c^{\prime\prime}\in C^{j}
is
presumed on the tensor product of complexes
C^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\otimes_{k}C^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
,
while the differential on the
k
-vector space
k
is zero.

A
left DG-comodule

M^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}=(M,d_{M})
over a DG-coalgebra
C^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}

is a complex of
k
-vector spaces endowed with a left
C
-comodule
structure such that the coaction map
\nu\colon M^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\longrightarrow C^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\otimes_{k}M^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
is a morphism of complexes of
k
-vector spaces.

Right DG-comodules
over
C^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
are defined similarly.

2.3.
Coalgebra structure on the bar-construction

Let
V
be a
k
-vector space.
Then the direct sum of the tensor powers of
V
,

k\oplus V\oplus(V\otimes_{k}V)\oplus(V\otimes_{k}V\otimes_{k}V)\oplus\dotsb\,\simeq\,\bigoplus\nolimits_{n=0}^{\infty}V^{\otimes n}

can be naturally endowed with a structure of graded associative
algebra over
k
.
The multiplication in this algebra, denoted by

T(V)=\bigoplus_{n=0}^{\infty}V^{\otimes n}
, is given by the rule

(v_{1}\otimes\dotsb\otimes v_{p})(w_{1}\otimes\dotsb\otimes w_{q})=v_{1}\otimes\dotsb\otimes v_{p}\otimes w_{1}\otimes\dotsb\otimes w_{q}

for all
v_{i}
,
w_{j}\in V
,
1\leq i\leq p
,
1\leq j\leq q
,
p
,
q\geq 0
.
The unit element in
T(V)
is
1\in k=V^{\otimes 0}
.
The algebra
T(V)
is the free associative, unital algebra spanned
by the vector space
V
.

The same graded
k
-vector space
\bigoplus_{n=0}^{\infty}V^{\otimes n}

also has a natural structure of graded coassociative coalgebra
over
k
.
The comultiplication in this coalgebra, denoted by

\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$T$}(V)=\bigoplus_{n=0}^{\infty}V^{\otimes n}
, is given by the rule

\mu(v_{1}\otimes\dotsb\otimes v_{n})=\sum\nolimits_{p+q=n}^{p,q\geq 0}(v_{1}\otimes\dotsb\otimes v_{p})\otimes(v_{p+1}\otimes\dotsb\otimes v_{p+q})

for all
v_{i}\in V
,
1\leq i\leq n
,
n\geq 0
.
The counit on
\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$T$}(V)
is the direct summand projection map

\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$T$}(V)\longrightarrow V^{\otimes 0}=k
.
We refer to Remark
3.2

for a discussion of a cofreeness property of the tensor coalgebra

\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$T$}(V)
.

Let
(A,\alpha)
be an augmented algebra over
k
.
We observe that the underlying graded vector space
\Br_{\alpha}(A)

of the bar-construction
\Br^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\alpha}(A)
, as defined in
Section
1.1
, coincides (up to a grading
sign change) with the graded
k
-vector space

\bigoplus_{n=0}^{\infty}A^{+}{}^{\otimes n}
.
Consequently, the graded vector space
\Br_{\alpha}(A)
has natural
structures of an associative algebra and a coassociative coalgebra.

It turns out that the bar differential
\partial
on
\Br^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\alpha}(A)

does
not
respect the multiplication on
\Br_{\alpha}(A)=T(A^{+})
,
i. e.,
\partial
does
not
satisfy any kind of Leibniz rule with
respect to the multiplication on
T(A^{+})
.
However, the differential
\partial
is compatible with the comultiplication
on
\Br_{\alpha}(A)=\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$T$}(A^{+})
.
In other words, the complex
\Br^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\alpha}(A)
endowed with the graded
coalgebra structure of
\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$T$}(A^{+})
is a
DG-coalgebra

in the sense of the definition in Section
2.2
.

2.4.
Derived Koszul duality formulated for complexes of modules

Let
(A,\alpha)
be an augmented
k
-algebra, and let
M^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
be
a complex of left
A
-modules.
We will denote simply by
M
the underlying graded
A
-module
of
M^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
.

Then the underlying graded vector space
\Br_{\alpha}(A,M)
of
the bar-construction
\Br^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\alpha}(A,M^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})
, as defined in
Sections
1.2
–
1.3
, can be viewed as
a cofree graded left comodule
\Br_{\alpha}(A,M)=\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$T$}(A^{+})\otimes_{k}M

over the tensor coalgebra
\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$T$}(A^{+})
(as explained in
Sections
2.1
–
2.2
).
It turns out that the total differential on
\Br^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\alpha}(A,M^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})

is compatible with the bar differential
\partial
on the DG-coalgebra

\Br^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\alpha}(A)
and the left coaction of
\Br_{\alpha}(A)
in

\Br_{\alpha}(A,M)
.
In other words, the complex
\Br^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\alpha}(A,M^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})
endowed with
the cofree graded comodule structure of
\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$T$}(A^{+})\otimes_{k}M
is
a
left DG-comodule
over the DG-coalgebra
\Br^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\alpha}(A)
.

The following theorem is our first formulation of the derived Koszul
duality.
At this point in our exposition, it is more of an advertisement than
a precise claim, in that it contains details which have not been
defined yet but will be defined below.

Theorem 2.1
.

Let
(A,\alpha)
be an augmented associative algebra over a field
k
.
Then the assignment
M^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\longmapsto\Br^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\alpha}(A,M^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})
induces
a triangulated equivalence between the derived category of left

A
-modules
\mathsf{D}(A{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}})
and the
coderived
category of
left DG-comodules over the DG-coalgebra
\Br^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\alpha}(A)
,

\mathsf{D}(A{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}})\simeq\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{co}}(\Br^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\alpha}(A){\operatorname{\mathsf{--comod}}}).

Proof.

This is a particular case of
[
64
, Theorem 6.3(a)]
;
see
[
47
, Théorème 2.2.2.2]
and
[
42
, Section 4]
for
an earlier approach.
The inverse functor,
\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{co}}(\Br^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\alpha}(A){\operatorname{\mathsf{--comod}}})\allowbreak\longrightarrow\mathsf{D}(A{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}})
, assigns to a left DG-comodule
N^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
over
the DG-coalgebra
C^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}=\Br^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\alpha}(A)
the graded left
A
-module

A\otimes_{k}N
endowed with a natural differential whose construction
will be explained in
Sections
5.3
–
5.4
.
The definition of the coderived category will be given in
Section
7.7
.
∎

The triangulated equivalence of
Theorem
2.1

takes the irreducible left
A
-module
k
(with the
A
-module
structure defined in terms of the augmentation
\alpha
) to
the cofree left DG-comodule
C^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
over the DG-coalgebra

C^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}=\Br^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\alpha}(A)
(cf. the discussion of cofree comodules
in Section
2.1
).
The same equivalence takes the left DG-comodule
k
over
C^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}

(with the
C
-comodule structure on
k
defined in terms of the unique
coaugmentation of
C
; see Sections
2.6

and
4.4
below) to
the free left
A
-module
A
.

For generalizations of
Theorem
2.1
,
see Theorems
2.4

and
5.4
below.

2.5.
Nonhomogeneous quadratic dual DG-coalgebra

The DG-coalgebra
\Br^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\alpha}(A)
produced by the bar-construction
is rather big.
In particular, it is essentially
never
finite-dimensional
(unless
A=k
).
The concept of
nonhomogeneous quadratic duality
, going back
to
[
45
]
,
[
83
, Section 3]
,
[
25
, Section 2.3]
,
and
[
61
]
, sometimes allows to produce a smaller DG-coalgebra
that can be used in lieu of
\Br^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\alpha}(A)
in Koszul duality
theorems such as
Theorem
2.1
.

Let
A=\bigoplus_{n=0}^{\infty}A_{n}
be a nonnegatively graded

k
-algebra with
A_{0}=k
.
Then the direct summand inclusion
A_{1}\longrightarrow A
can be uniquely
extended to a morphism of graded algebras
\pi\colon T(A_{1})\longrightarrow A
.
The algebra
A
is said to be
quadratic
if
the homomorphism
\pi
is surjective and its kernel is generated by
elements of degree
2
(simply speaking, this means that the algebra
A

is generated by elements of degree
1
with relations in degree
2
).

The graded algebra
A
is called
Koszul
if
\Tor^{A}_{ij}(k,k)=0

for all
i\neq j
.
Here the first grading
i
on the
\Tor
spaces is the usual
homological grading, while the second grading
j
, called
the
internal
grading, is induced by the grading of
A
.
This condition for
i=1
means precisely that the algebra
A
is
generated by
A_{1}
; assuming this is the case, the same condition
for
i=2
means precisely that
A
is defined by quadratic relations.
So any Koszul graded algebra is quadratic
[
58
, Corollary 5.3 in
Chapter 1]
.

Let
A=\bigoplus_{n=0}^{\infty}A_{n}
be a quadratic algebra.
Put
V=A_{1}
, and denote by
I\subset V\otimes_{k}V
the kernel of
the (surjective) multiplication map
A_{1}\otimes_{k}A_{1}\longrightarrow A_{2}
.
So
V
is the space of generators of the quadratic algebra
A
,
and
I
is the space of defining quadratic relations of
A
.
The grading components of
A
can be expressed in terms of
V
and
I

by the formula

A_{n}=V^{\otimes n}\Big/\sum\nolimits_{i=1}^{n-1}V^{\otimes i-1}\otimes_{k}I\otimes_{k}V^{\otimes n-i-1},\qquad n\geq 1.

Consider the tensor coalgebra
\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$T$}(V)
, and denote by
C=A^{?}

the graded subcoalgebra in
\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$T$}(V)
with the components
C^{0}=k
,
C^{1}=V
,
C^{2}=I
, and generally

C^{n}=\bigcap\nolimits_{i=1}^{n-1}V^{\otimes i-1}\otimes_{k}I\otimes_{k}V^{\otimes n-i-1}\,\subset\,V^{\otimes n},\qquad n\geq 1.

The coalgebra
C
is called the
quadratic dual coalgebra
to
a quadratic algebra
A

[
82
, Section 2.1]
.

Remark 2.2
.

In most expositions, including the present author’s
[
61
]

and
[
58
]
, the quadratic duality is viewed as a contravariant
functor constructed using the passage to the dual vector space.
The contravariant duality assigns to a quadratic algebra
A

the quadratic algebra
A^{!}
, which can be obtained as the graded dual

k
-vector space to the coalgebra
C=A^{?}
, that is,
A^{!}_{n}=(C^{n})^{*}

for all
n\geq 0
.
The construction of the quadratic algebra
A^{!}
works well for
quadratic algebras
A
with finite-dimensional components (see,
e. g.,
[
58
, Section 3 in Chapter 1]
), and it can be made to work
without the assumption of locally finite dimension by considering
linearly compact topological vector spaces.
Our general preference is to use coalgebras instead, and consider
the covariant quadratic duality between algebras and coalgebras as
constructed above.
In particular, the intended applications in
[
82
]
were quadratic
algebras with infinite-dimensional components; that is one reason
why the covariant algebra-coalgebra quadratic duality was introduced
in
[
82
]
.
In the context of derived nonhomogeneous Koszul duality over a field,
which is the subject of this survey, the language of covariant duality
is more illuminating, in our view.

Let
0=F_{-1}A\subset k=F_{0}A\subset F_{1}A\subset F_{2}A\subset\dotsb
be
a
k
-algebra endowed with an increasing filtration
F
such that

A=\bigcup_{n\geq 0}F_{n}A
,
1\in F_{0}A
, and
F_{n}A\cdot F_{m}A\subset F_{n+m}A
for all
n
,
m\geq 0
.
Then the associated graded vector space
\mathrm{gr}^{F}A=\bigoplus_{n=0}^{\infty}F_{n}A/F_{n-1}A
carries a naturally induced associative algebra
structure.
A
nonhomogeneous quadratic algebra
is a filtered algebra

(A,F)
such that the graded algebra
\mathrm{gr}^{F}A
is quadratic.
A
nonhomogeneous Koszul algebra
is a filtered algebra

(A,F)
such that the graded algebra
\mathrm{gr}^{F}A
is
Koszul
[
83
, Section 2]
,
[
58
, Chapter 5]
.

Example 2.3
.

(1) Let
V
be a
k
-vector space.
Consider the graded algebra
A=\bigoplus_{n=0}^{\infty}A_{n}
with
the components
A_{0}=k
,
A_{1}=V
, and
A_{n}=0
for all
n\geq 2
.
The multiplication on the graded algebra
A
is trivial:
the multiplication map
A_{n}\otimes A_{m}\longrightarrow A_{n+m}
vanishes
for all
n
,
m>0
(in the cases when
n=0
or
m=0

the multiplication map is determined by the condition that

1\in k=A_{0}
is the unit element of
A
).
The graded algebra
A
is well-known to be Koszul (see, e. g.,

[
58
, Corollary 2.4 or 4.3 in Chapter 2]
for much more
general results).
In this example, the quadratic dual coalgebra
C=A^{?}
is the whole
tensor coalgebra, that is,
C=\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$T$}(V)
.

(2) Any nonzero associative algebra
A
can be endowed with
the
trivial
filtration,
F_{0}A=k\cdot 1
and
F_{1}A=A
.
Then the associated graded algebra
\mathrm{gr}^{F}A
has grading components

\mathrm{gr}^{F}_{0}A=k
,
\mathrm{gr}^{F}_{1}A=A/(k\cdot\penalty 1)
, and
\mathrm{gr}^{F}_{n}A=0

for
n\geq 2
.
So the algebra
\mathrm{gr}^{F}A
has the form of example (1) above (with

V=A/(k\cdot\penalty 1)
), and the multiplication on it is trivial in
the sense we have explained.
Thus the passage to the associated graded algebra of an algebra
A

with respect to the trivial filtration
F
destroys all the information
about the multiplication in
A
.

It is clear from this discussion that
any
nonzero associative
algebra
A
endowed with the trivial filtration
F
is a nonhomogeneous
Koszul algebra.
Some associative algebras
A
admit nontrivial filtrations making them
nonhomogeneous Koszul algebras with a more interesting associated graded
algebra
\mathrm{gr}^{F}A
and a smaller quadratic dual coalgebra
C=(\mathrm{gr}^{F}A)^{?}

(cf. Example
2.5
below).

Let
(A,F)
be a nonhomogeneous quadratic algebra and
C=(\mathrm{gr}^{F}A)^{?}

be the quadratic dual coalgebra to the quadratic algebra
\mathrm{gr}^{F}A
.
Assume additionally that the algebra
A
is endowed with
an augmentation
\alpha\colon A\longrightarrow k
with the augmentation ideal

A^{+}\subset A
.
Then there is a natural isomorphism
\mathrm{gr}^{F}_{1}A=F_{1}A/F_{0}A\simeq A^{+}\cap F_{1}A\subset A
.
So the vector space
V=\mathrm{gr}^{F}_{1}A
can be viewed as a subspace in
A^{+}
,
and the graded coalgebra
C
can be viewed as a subcoalgebra in

\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$T$}(A^{+})
,

C\subset\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$T$}(V)\subset\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$T$}(A^{+})=\Br_{\alpha}(A).

One observes that the subcoalgebra
C\subset\Br_{\alpha}(A)
is
in fact a DG-subcoalgebra
C^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\subset\Br^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\alpha}(A)
, that is

\partial(C)\subset C
.
Essentially, the first reason for that is because the multiplication
map
A^{+}\otimes_{k}A^{+}\longrightarrow A^{+}
restricted to the subspace

I\subset\mathrm{gr}^{F}_{1}A\otimes_{k}\mathrm{gr}^{F}_{1}A\simeq(A^{+}\cap F_{1}A)\otimes_{k}(A^{+}\cap F_{1}A)\subset A^{+}\otimes_{k}A^{+}

lands within the subspace
A^{+}\cap F_{1}A\subset A^{+}
(by the definition
of
I
as the kernel of the multiplication map
\mathrm{gr}^{F}_{1}A\otimes_{k}\mathrm{gr}^{F}_{1}A\longrightarrow\mathrm{gr}^{F}_{2}A=F_{2}A/F_{1}A
in the graded algebra
\mathrm{gr}^{F}A
).
Further details need to be checked; we refer
to
[
83
, Section 3]
,
[
61
, Proposition 2.2]

or
[
58
, Proposition 4.1 in Chapter 5]
.
A generalization to nonaugmented algebras
A
will be discussed in
Example
6.11
below.

Let
(A,F,\alpha)
be an augmented nonhomogeneous quadratic algebra
and
M^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
be a complex of left
A
-modules.
Then one can easily see that
C\otimes_{k}M\subset\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$T$}(A^{+})\otimes_{k}M=\Br_{\alpha}(A,M)
is a subcomplex in
\Br^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\alpha}(A,M)
, that is

\partial(C\otimes_{k}M)\subset C\otimes_{k}M
.
In anticipation of the discussion in
Section
5
, we denote the complex
C\otimes_{k}M

with the differential induced by the differential
\partial
on

\Br^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\alpha}(A,M)
by
C^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\otimes^{\tau}M^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
.
This notation is indented to emphasize the fact that the differential
on
C^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\otimes^{\tau}M^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
is
not
simply the tensor product
differential on the tensor product of two complexes
C^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\otimes_{k}M^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
,
but rather some twisted version of it taking the action of
A
in
M

into account.

Let
(A,F,\alpha)
be a nonhomogeneous Koszul algebra.
Then the DG-coalgebra
C^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
is quasi-isomorphic to the ambient
DG-coalgebra
\Br^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\alpha}(A)
, and the complex
C^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\otimes^{\tau}M^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
is quasi-isomorphic to the ambient bar-complex

\Br^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\alpha}(A,M)
.
Accordingly, one has

H^{-n}(C^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})\simeq\Tor_{n}^{A}(k,k)\quad\text{and}\quad H^{-n}(C^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\otimes^{\tau}M)\simeq\Tor_{n}^{A}(k,M)

for any left
A
-module
M
and all
n\geq 0
.

Theorem 2.4
.

Let
(A,F,\alpha)
be an augmented nonhomogeneous Koszul algebra
over a field
k
.
Then the assignment
M^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\longmapsto C^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\otimes^{\tau}M^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
induces
a triangulated equivalence between the derived category of left

A
-modules
\mathsf{D}(A{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}})
and the
coderived
category of left
DG-comodules over the DG-coalgebra
C^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
,

\mathsf{D}(A{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}})\simeq\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{co}}(C^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--comod}}}).

Proof.

This is a particular case of
[
64
, Theorems 6.5(a) and 6.6]
.
The inverse functor,
\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{co}}(C^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--comod}}})\longrightarrow\mathsf{D}(A{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}})
, assigns
to a left DG-comodule
N^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
over
C^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
the complex of left

A
-modules
A\otimes^{\tau}N^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
, which means the graded left
A
-module

A\otimes_{k}N
endowed with a twisted differential, as explained below
in Sections
5.3
–
5.4
.
The definition of the coderived category will be given
in Section
7.7
.
∎

Any associative algebra
A
can be viewed as a nonhomogeneous Koszul
algebra with the trivial filtration, as explained in
Example
2.3
(2).
For an augmented algebra
A
endowed with the trivial filtration,
one has
C^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}=\Br^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\alpha}(A)
.
So Theorem
2.1

can be obtained as a particular case of
Theorem
2.4
for
the trivial filtration
F
.

For a generalization of
Theorem
2.4
, see
Theorem
5.4
below.

Example 2.5
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a Lie algebra over a field
k
and
\Lambda(\mathfrak{g})
be
the exterior algebra spanned by the
k
-vector space
\mathfrak{g}
.
Then the Chevalley–Eilenberg differential
\partial
on
\Lambda(\mathfrak{g})

makes
\Lambda(\mathfrak{g})
a complex computing the homology spaces

H_{*}(\mathfrak{g},k)
of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
with coefficients in
the trivial
\mathfrak{g}
-module
k

[
17
, Chapter III]
,
[
45
]
.
The differential
\partial
does not respect the exterior multiplication
on
\Lambda(\mathfrak{g})
, but it is an odd coderivation of the exterior
comultiplication; so
C^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}=(\Lambda(\mathfrak{g}),\partial)
is a DG-coalgebra.

The enveloping algebra
U(\mathfrak{g})
is endowed with the natural
(Poincaré–Birkhoff–Witt) filtration
F
, making
U(\mathfrak{g})

(the thematic example of) a nonhomogeneous Koszul algebra over
a field.
There is also a natural augmentation
\alpha
on
U(\mathfrak{g})
,
corresponding to the action of
\mathfrak{g}
in trivial
\mathfrak{g}
-modules.
The construction above assigns to the augmented nonhomogeneous
quadratic algebra
(U(g),F,\alpha)
the DG-coalgebra

(\Lambda(\mathfrak{g}),\partial)
.

Moreover, for any
\mathfrak{g}
-module
M
, the homological
Chevalley–Eilenberg complex
(\Lambda(\mathfrak{g})\otimes_{k}M,\>\partial)
of
the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
with coefficients in
M
is a DG-comodule
over the
(\Lambda(\mathfrak{g}),\partial)
.
The construction above assigns the DG-comodule
C^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\otimes^{\tau}M=(\Lambda(\mathfrak{g})\otimes_{k}M,\>\partial)
to the
U(\mathfrak{g})
-module
M
.

Thus Theorem
2.4

claims that the construction of the homological Chevalley–Eilenberg
complex induces an equivalence between the derived category of

\mathfrak{g}
-modules and the coderived category of DG-comodules over
the DG-coalgebra
(\Lambda(\mathfrak{g}),\partial)
.
For a finite-dimensional Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, the coalgebra

\Lambda(\mathfrak{g})
is finite-dimensional; so one can pass to the dual
algebra
\Lambda(\mathfrak{g}^{*})
and say that the construction of
the cohomological Chevalley–Eilenberg complex

(\Lambda(\mathfrak{g}^{*})\otimes_{k}M,\>d)
induces an equivalence between
the derived category of
\mathfrak{g}
-modules and the coderived category
of DG-modules over the DG-algebra

\Lambda^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(\mathfrak{g}^{*})=(\Lambda(\mathfrak{g}^{*}),d)
,

(1)

\mathsf{D}(\mathfrak{g}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}})\simeq\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{co}}(\Lambda^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(\mathfrak{g}^{*}){\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}).

This example can be found in
[
64
, Example 6.6]
.

Let us emphasize that these assertions are certainly
not

true for the conventional derived category of DG-(co)modules
in place of the coderived category.
In fact, the derived category
\mathsf{D}(\mathfrak{g}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}})
is
not

equivalent to the derived category
\mathsf{D}(\Lambda^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(\mathfrak{g}^{*}){\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}})

already when
\mathfrak{g}
is a finite-dimensional semisimple Lie algebra
over a field
k
of characteristic
0
, or indeed, a one-dimensional
abelian Lie algebra (cf. Example
1.2
(2)).
We will continue this discussion in
Example
7.3
below.

A relative version of
Theorem
2.4
, with
the ground field
k
replaced by an arbitrary associative ring
R
,
can be found in the book
[
75
]
.
See
[
75
, Sections 3.8 and 6.6]
.

2.6.
Cobar-construction; DG-algebra and DG-module structures

Let
C
be a (coassociative, counital) coalgebra over a field
k
,
as defined in Section
2.1
.
A
coaugmentation

\gamma
on
C
is a (counital) coalgebra
homomorphism
\gamma\colon k\longrightarrow C
.
So the composition
k\overset{\gamma}{\longrightarrow}C\overset{\epsilon}{\longrightarrow}k

is the identity map.
We denote by
C^{+}=\coker(\gamma)
the cokernel of the augmentation map.
Generally speaking,
C^{+}
is a coassociative coalgebra without counit
(dually to the augmentation ideal, which is an associative algebra
without unit).
The coalgebra homomorphism
\gamma\colon k\longrightarrow C
endows
the one-dimensional
k
-vector space
k
with left and right

C
-comodule structures.

The
cobar-construction

\Cb^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\gamma}(C)
of a coaugmented
coalgebra
C=(C,\gamma)
is defined as
the complex
[
20
, Chapter II]
,
[
1
]

k\overset{0}{\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5mu}C^{+}\overset{\partial}{\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5mu}C^{+}\otimes_{k}C^{+}\overset{\partial}{\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5mu}C^{+}\otimes_{k}C^{+}\otimes_{k}C^{+}\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5mu\dotsb

with the differential given by the formulas
\partial(c)=\mu^{+}(c)
,
\partial(c_{1}\otimes c_{2})=\mu^{+}(c_{1})\otimes c_{2}-c_{1}\otimes\mu^{+}(c_{2})
, …,

\partial(c_{1}\otimes\dotsb\otimes c_{n})=\mu^{+}(c_{1})\otimes c_{2}\otimes\dotsb\otimes c_{n}-\dotsb\\
+(-1)^{i-1}c_{1}\otimes\dotsb\otimes c_{i-1}\otimes\mu^{+}(c_{i})\otimes c_{i+1}\otimes\dotsb\otimes c_{n}+\dotsb+(-1)^{n-1}c_{1}\otimes\dotsb\otimes c_{n-1}\otimes\mu^{+}(c_{n}),

etc., for all
c
,
c_{i}\in C^{+}
,
1\leq i\leq n
, and
n\geq 1
.
Here
\mu^{+}\colon C^{+}\longrightarrow C^{+}\otimes_{k}C^{+}
is the comultiplication map
on
C^{+}
(induced by the comultiplication map
\mu\colon C\longrightarrow C\otimes_{k}C
).
The leftmost differential
\partial\colon k\longrightarrow C^{+}
is the zero map.

Let
(C,\gamma)
be a coaugmented coalgebra and
M
be a left

C
-comodule.
The
cobar-construction

\Cb^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\gamma}(C,M)
of
C
with
the coefficients in
M
is the complex

M\overset{\partial}{\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5mu}C^{+}\otimes_{k}M\overset{\partial}{\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5mu}C^{+}\otimes_{k}C^{+}\otimes_{k}M\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5mu\dotsb

with the differentials given by the formulas
\partial(m)=\nu^{+}(m)
,
\partial(c\otimes m)=\mu^{+}(c)\otimes m-c\otimes\nu^{+}(m)
, …,

\partial(c_{1}\otimes\dotsb\otimes c_{n}\otimes m)=\mu^{+}(c_{1})\otimes c_{2}\otimes\dotsb\otimes c_{n}\otimes m-\dotsb\\
+(-1)^{n-1}c_{1}\otimes\dotsb\otimes c_{n-1}\otimes\mu^{+}(c_{n})\otimes m+(-1)^{n}c_{1}\otimes\dotsb\otimes c_{n}\otimes\nu^{+}(m),

etc., for all
c_{i}\in C^{+}
,
1\leq i\leq n
, and
n\geq 0
.
Here
\nu^{+}\colon M\longrightarrow C^{+}\otimes_{k}M
is the left
C^{+}
-coaction map
obtained by composing the left
C
-coaction map
\nu\colon M\longrightarrow C\otimes_{k}M
with the natural surjection
C\otimes_{k}M\longrightarrow C^{+}\otimes_{k}M
.

Dually to the discussion in
Sections
2.3
–
2.4
,
one observes that the underlying graded vector space
\Cb_{\gamma}(C)

of the cobar-construction
\Cb^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\gamma}(C)
coincides with
the graded
k
-vector space
\bigoplus_{n=0}^{\infty}C^{+}{}^{\otimes n}
.
Consequently, the graded vector space
\Cb_{\gamma}(C)
has natural
structures of an associative algebra and a coassociative coalgebra.

It turns out that the cobar differential
\partial
on
\Cb^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\gamma}(C)

does
not
respect the comultiplication on
\Cb_{\gamma}(C)=\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$T$}(C^{+})
.
However, the differential
\partial
is compatible with the multiplication on

\Cb_{\gamma}(C)=T(C^{+})
, i. e., it satisfies the Leibniz rule with signs

\partial(ab)=\partial(a)b+(-1)^{|a|}a\partial(b)

for any elements
a
and
b\in T(C^{+})
of degrees
|a|
and
|b|
.
In other words, the complex
\Cb^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\gamma}(C)
endowed with
the graded algebra structure of
T(C^{+})
is a DG-algebra over
k
.

The graded algebra of cohomology of the DG-algebra
\Cb^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\gamma}(C)

is naturally isomorphic to (the graded algebra with the opposite
multiplication to) the Ext algebra of the left
C
-comodule
k
,

H^{*}\Cb^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\gamma}(C)\simeq\Ext_{C}^{*}(k,k),

where
k
is endowed with the left
C
-comodule structure via
\gamma

and the Ext is computed in the abelian category of left
C
-comodules.
Similarly, for any left
C
-comodule
M
, the graded module of
cohomology of the DG-module
\Cb^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\gamma}(C,M)
over
the DG-algebra
\Cb^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\gamma}(C)
(see the discussion in the next
Section
2.7
)
is naturally isomorphic to the Ext module
\Ext_{C}^{*}(k,M)
over
the algebra
\Ext_{C}^{*}(k,k)
,

H^{*}\Cb^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\gamma}(C,M)\simeq\Ext_{C}^{*}(k,M).

2.7.
Derived Koszul duality formulated for complexes of comodules

Let
N^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
be a complex of left
C
-comodules.
Then the cobar-construction
\Cb^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\gamma}(C,N^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})
is a bicomplex
of
k
-vector spaces.
We totalize this bicomplex by taking infinite direct sums along
the diagonals.

Denote simply by
N
the underlying graded
C
-comodule of
a complex of left
C
-comodules
N^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
.
The underlying graded vector space
\Cb_{\gamma}(C,N)
of
the cobar-construction
\Cb^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\gamma}(C,N^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})
can be viewed as
a free graded left module
T(C^{+})\otimes_{k}N
over the tensor algebra

T(C^{+})
.
It turns out that the total differential on
\Cb^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\gamma}(C,N^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})

is compatible with the cobar differential
\partial
on the DG-algebra

\Cb^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(C)
and the left action of
\Cb_{\gamma}(C)
in

\Cb_{\gamma}(C,N)
; i. e., a Leibniz rule with signs similar to
the one in Section
2.6
is satisfied
for this action.
In other words, the complex
\Cb^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\gamma}(C,N^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})
endowed with
the free graded module structure of
T(C^{+})\otimes_{k}N
is
a left DG-module over the DG-algebra
\Cb^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\gamma}(C)
.

We formulate two versions of the derived Koszul duality for
complexes of comodules, the nonconilpotent and the conilpotent one.
The definitions of the coderived and the absolute derived categories
will be given in Sections
7.6

and
7.7
.

Theorem 2.6
.

Let
(C,\gamma)
be a coaugmented coassociative coalgebra over
a field
k
.
Then the assignment
N^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\longmapsto\Cb^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\gamma}(C,N^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})

induces a triangulated equivalence between the
coderived

category of left
C
-comodules
\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{co}}(C{\operatorname{\mathsf{--comod}}})
and
the
absolute derived
category of left DG-modules over
the DG-algebra
\Cb^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\gamma}(C)
,

\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{co}}(C{\operatorname{\mathsf{--comod}}})\simeq\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\Cb^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\gamma}(C){\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}).

Proof.

This is a particular case of
[
64
, Theorem 6.7(a)]
.
The absolute derived category
\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\Cb^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\gamma}(C){\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}})

coincides with the coderived category
\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{co}}(\Cb^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\gamma}(C){\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}})

by
[
64
, Theorem 3.6(a)]
; see
Theorem
7.8
(a) below.
∎

Theorem 2.7
.

Let
C
be a
conilpotent
coassociative coalgebra over
a field
k
, endowed with its unique coaugmentation
\gamma
.
Then the same assignment
N^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\longmapsto\Cb^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\gamma}(C,N^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})

induces a triangulated equivalence between the
coderived

category of left
C
-comodules
\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{co}}(C{\operatorname{\mathsf{--comod}}})
and
the conventional
derived
category of left DG-modules over
the DG-algebra
\Cb^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\gamma}(C)
,

\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{co}}(C{\operatorname{\mathsf{--comod}}})\simeq\mathsf{D}(\Cb^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\gamma}(C){\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}).

Proof.

This is a particular case of
[
64
, Theorem 6.4(a)]
;
see
[
47
, Théorème 2.2.2.2]
and
[
42
, Section 4]
for
an earlier approach.
It will be explained in the next
Section
3
what
a “conilpotent coalgebra” is.
∎

In both the theorems, the inverse functor assigns to a left
DG-module
M^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
over the DG-algebra
A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}=\Cb^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\gamma}(C)

the complex of left
C
-comodules
C\otimes^{\tau}M^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
, which is
a notation for the graded left
C
-comodule
C\otimes_{k}M
endowed
with a twisted differential, as constructed below in
Sections
5.3
–
5.4
.

In both Theorems
2.6

and
2.7
,
the triangulated equivalence takes the irreducible left

C
-comodule
k
(with the
C
-comodule structure defined in terms
of the coaugmentation
\gamma
) to the free left DG-module
A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}

over the DG-algebra
A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}=\Cb^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\gamma}(C)
.
The same equivalences take the left DG-module
k
over
A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}

(with the
A
-module structure on
k
defined in terms of the natural
augmentation of
A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
; see
Section
4.4
) to
the cofree (injective) left
C
-comodule
C
.

For generalizations of
Theorems
2.6
–
2.7
, see
Section
5.4
below.

Remark 2.8
.

One can obtain the definition of a coalgebra from the definition
of an algebra over a field by inverting the arrows, but inverting
the arrows in the formulation of
Theorem
2.1

does not exactly produce the formulation of
Theorem
2.6

or
2.7
.
The roles of algebras and coalgebras in derived nonhomogeneous
Koszul duality are
not
symmetric up to inverting the arrows,
as there are subtle differences between the constructions on
the algebra and coalgebra side.

Of course, one can say that the associative algebras are the monoid
objects in the monoidal (tensor) category of vector spaces, while
the coassociative coalgebras are the monoid objects in the monoidal
category opposite to vector spaces.
Then the point is that the monoidal category of infinite-dimensional
vector spaces is
not
equivalent to its opposite monoidal
category, and moreover, the properties of these categories are different
in a way important for nonhomogeneous Koszul duality.

To begin with, the tensor product of vector spaces preserves infinite
direct sums, but not infinite products.
Furthermore, the functor of tensor product of vector spaces (with one
of the two objects fixed) has a right adjoint, but not a left adjoint
functor.
It is also relevant that the (filtered) direct limits of vector spaces
are exact functors, but the inverse limits aren’t.
These differences cause the lack of symmetry between the Koszul
duality theorems formulated above and in
Sections
2.4
–
2.5
.

In particular, it is illuminating to observe that the constructions of
the DG-comodule
\Br^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\alpha}(A,M^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})
and the DG-module

\Cb^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\gamma}(C,N^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})
are
not
transformed into one another
by inverting the arrows, for the subtle reason that the direct sum
(rather than the direct product) totalization of the respective
bicomplex is used in
both
the constructions.
The fact that the tensor products preserve the direct sums, but not
the direct products of vector spaces explains this choice of
the totalization procedures, but it breaks the symmetry between
the algebra and coalgebra sides.

3.
Structure Theory of Coalgebras; Conilpotency

All coassociative coalgebras over a field are unions of their
finite-dimensional subcoalgebras.
For this reason, the structure theory of coalgebras stands
approximately on the same level of complexity as the structure
theory of finite-dimensional algebras.
So it is much simpler than the structure theory of infinite-dimensional
algebras and rings.
Some details of this argument are elaborated upon in this section.

3.1.
Brief remarks about coalgebras

The theory of coalgebras is notoriously counterintuitive.
Most algebraists seem to find it difficult to invert the arrows
properly in their mind in order to pass from the familiar ring theory
to the coalgebra theory.
One needs to get used to that.

Here we only mention a couple of obvious points.
In ring theory, subrings are aplenty and quotient rings are rare.
To pass to the quotient ring, one needs to have an ideal in
the ring.
All ideals are subalgebras (without unit), but most nonunital
subalgebras are not ideals.
Furthermore, all two-sided ideals are both left ideals and right
ideals, but most left or right ideals are not (two-sided) ideals.

In coalgebras, the situation is reversed.
A quotient coalgebra is the dual concept to a subalgebra, and
a subcoalgebra is the dual concept to a quotient algebra.
Quotient coalgebras are aplenty, but subcoalgebras are rare.

To pass to the quotient coalgebra, it suffices to have a coideal
in the coalgebra.
If one is satisfied with obtaning a noncounital quotient coalgebra,
then the definition of a coideal reduces to this: a vector subspace
J

in a coalgebra
C
is said to be a
coideal
(in the noncounital
sense) if
\mu(J)\subset J\otimes_{k}C+C\otimes_{k}J
(where

\mu\colon C\longrightarrow C\otimes_{k}C
is the comultiplication map).
The definition of a coideal in the counital sense includes
an additional condition that the counit
\epsilon\colon C\longrightarrow k

must vanish on a coideal
J
.

For comparison, a
subcoalgebra

D\subset C
is a vector
subspace such that
\mu(D)\subset D\otimes_{k}D
.
One immediately observes that any subcoalgebra is a coideal (in
the noncounital sense), but most coideals are not subcoalgebras.
Furthermore, any left coideal (i. e., a left subcomodule in
the left
C
-comodule
C
) is a two-sided coideal, and any right
coideal is a two-sided coideal (in the noncounital sense), but most
(two-sided) coideals are neither left nor right coideals.

It is also worth mentioning that the comodule structures induced
via homomorphisms of coalgebras are pushed forward rather than
pulled back.
So if
f\colon C\longrightarrow D
is a homomorphism of coalgebras, then any
(left or right)
C
-comodule acquires the induced structure of
a left
D
-comodule.
The resulting exact, faithful functor
C{\operatorname{\mathsf{--comod}}}\longrightarrow D{\operatorname{\mathsf{--comod}}}

on the abelian categories of comodules is called
the
corestriction of scalars
with respect to
f
.

We refer to
[
88
, Section 1.4]
for further details.

3.2.
Local finite-dimensionality

The following classical result can be found
in
[
88
, Corollary 2.1.4 and Theorem 2.2.1]
.

Lemma 3.1
.

(a)
Any coassociative coalgebra
C
is the (directed) union of
its finite-dimensional subcoalgebras.
The sum of any two finite-dimensional subcoalgebras in
C
is
a finite-dimensional subcoalgebra in
C
.

(b)
Any (left) comodule over a coassociative coalgebra
C

is the union of its finite-dimensional
C
-subcomodules.

(c)
Any finite-dimensional
C
-comodule
M
is a comodule
over some finite-dimensional subcoalgebra
E\subset C
(so the image
of the coaction map
\nu\colon M\longrightarrow C\otimes_{k}M
is contained in
E\otimes_{k}M
).

Proof.

Let us sketch a proof of the first assertion of part (a); the other
assertions are similar or simpler.
Given a coassociative, counital coalgebra
C
, consider an arbitrary
finite-dimensional vector subspace
V\subset C
, and denote by

E\subset C
the full preimage of
C\otimes_{k}V\otimes_{k}C
under the iterated
comultiplication map
\mu^{(2)}=(\mu\otimes\mathrm{id}_{C})\circ\mu=(\mathrm{id}_{C}\otimes\mu)\circ\mu\colon C\longrightarrow C\otimes_{k}C\otimes_{k}C
.
Then
E=E(V)
is a subcoalgebra in
C
, one has
E\subset V
(so
E
is
finite-dimensional), and
C=\bigcup_{V\subset C}E(V)
.
∎

Specifying a coalgebra structure on a finite-dimensional
k
-vector
space
E
is equivalent to specifying an algebra structure on the dual
vector space
E^{*}
.
So the dual vector space to a finite-dimensional coalgebra is
a finite-dimensional algebra, and the dual vector space to
a finite-dimensional algebra is a finite-dimensional coalgebra.
For infinite-dimensional vector spaces the situation is more
complicated: the dual vector space to a coalgebra has a natural
algebra structure, but
not
vice versa.

3.3.
Conilpotent coalgebras

A nonunital associative algebra
A
is said to be
nilpotent

if there exists an integer
n\geq 1
such that the product of any

n
elements in
A
vanishes, that is
A^{n}=0
.
An augmented algebra
A
is said to be
nilpotent
if its
augmentation ideal
A^{+}
is a nilpotent algebra without unit, that is,
the product of any
n
elements in
A^{+}
vanishes.

Notice that the augmentation of a nilpotent augmented algebra
A

is
unique
.
Since the product of any
n
elements in
A^{+}
vanishes, there are
no nonzero
k
-algebra homomorphisms
A^{+}\longrightarrow k
; hence only one
unital
k
-algebra homomorphism
A\longrightarrow k
.

Coalgebras are better suited for nilpotency than algebras, in the sense
that one can speak of a coalgebra being conilpotent without assuming
existence of a related finite integer
n
.
One can say that a (unital or nonunital) finite-dimensional coalgebra

E
is
conilpotent
if its dual finite-dimensional algebra is
nilpotent.
Then an arbitrary coalgebra
C
is said to be
conilpotent
if
all its finite-dimensional subcoalgebras are conilpotent, i. e.,
C

is a union of finite-dimensional conilpotent coalgebras.
So a finite-dimensional conilpotent coalgebra always has a finite
conilpotency degree, but an infinite-dimensional conilpotent coalgebra
need
not
have it.

Here is an explicit definition of a conilpotent coalgebra without
counit that is easy to work with on the technical level.
A noncounital coalgebra
D
is said to be
conilpotent
if
for every element
x\in D
there exists an integer
n\geq 1
such that
the image of
x
under the iterated comultiplication map

\mu^{(n)}\colon D\longrightarrow D^{\otimes n+1}
vanishes.
A coaugmented coalgebra
(C,\gamma)
is called
conilpotent
if
the noncounital coalgebra
C^{+}=C/\gamma(k)
is conilpotent in
the sense of the previous definition.
A conilpotent coaugmented coalgebra has a unique coaugmentation;
moreover, any counital coalgebra homomorphism between two conilpotent
coalgebras preserves the coaugmentations.

A conilpotent coalgebra
C
comes endowed with a natural increasing
filtration
F
.
Namely,
F_{-1}C=0
,
F_{0}C=\gamma(k)\simeq k
, and for every
n\geq 0

the subspace
F_{n}C\subset C
consists of all elements
c\in C
whose
image in
(C^{+})^{\otimes n+1}
under the composition of maps

C\longrightarrow C^{\otimes n+1}\longrightarrow(C^{+})^{\otimes n+1}
vanishes.
Then one has
\mu(F_{n}C)\subset\sum_{p+q=n}F_{p}C\otimes_{k}F_{q}C\subset C\otimes_{k}C
, so the filtration
F
on
C
is compatible with
the comultiplication.
The equation
C=\bigcup_{n\geq 0}F_{n}C
expresses the condition that

C
is conilpotent.

A noncounital conilpotent coalgebra
D
comes endowed with a similarly
defined natural increasing filtration
F
.
Namely, one has
F_{0}D=0
, and for every
n\geq 0
the subspace

F_{n}D\subset D
is the kernel of the iterated comultiplication map

\mu^{(n)}\colon D\longrightarrow D^{\otimes n+1}
.
Then one has
\mu(F_{n}D)\subset\sum_{p+q=n}F_{p}D\otimes_{k}F_{q}D\subset D\otimes_{k}D
and
D=\bigcup_{n\geq 1}F_{n}D
.

Remark 3.2
.

Let
V
be a
k
-vector space.
In the discussion of the tensor algebra
T(V)
and tensor coalgebra

\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$T$}(V)
in Section
2.3
,
it was mentioned that
T(V)
is the free associative algebra spanned
by
V
, but a discussion of a possible cofreeness property of
the tensor coalgebra
\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$T$}(V)
was postponed.
Now it is the time for such a discussion.

The first important observation is that the coaugmented coalgebra

\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$T$}(V)
is always conilpotent.
In fact, one has
F_{n}\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$T$}(V)=\bigoplus_{i=0}^{n}V^{\otimes i}
for
every
n\geq 0
.
Moreover,
\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$T$}(V)
is the cofree conilpotent coalgebra cospanned
by
V
, in the following sense.
Let
C
be a conilpotent coalgebra, and let
f\colon C\longrightarrow V
be
a
k
-linear map annihilating the coaugmentation, i. e., such that
the composition
f\gamma\colon k\longrightarrow V
vanishes.
Then there exists a unique coalgebra homomorphism
t\colon C\longrightarrow\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$T$}(V)

whose composition with the direct summand projection
\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$T$}(V)\longrightarrow V^{\otimes 1}=V
is equal to
f
.

Let us emphasize that the assertion above is
not
true for
nonconilpotent coalgebras
C
.
For such coalgebras, a homomorphism like
t
does not exist in general.

3.4.
Cosemisimple coalgebras and the coradical filtration

The classical structure theory of finite-dimensional associative
algebras over a field tells that any such algebra
A
has
a nilpotent Jacobson radical
J
, while the quotient algebra
A/J

decomposes uniquely as a finite product of simple algebras.
The Jacobson radical
J\subset A
is simultaneously the unique maximal
nilpotent ideal and the unique minimal ideal in
A
for which
the quotient algebra
A/J
is semisimple.

Moreover, if
f\colon A\longrightarrow B
is a surjective homomorphism of
finite-dimensional
k
-algebras, then the Jacobson radicals
J(A)\subset A
and
J(B)\subset B
are well-behaved with respect to
f

in the sense that
f(J(A))=J(B)
.
The induced surjective homomorphism of semisimple algebras

A/J(A)\longrightarrow B/J(B)
is a projection onto a direct factor; in fact,
it represents the algebra
B/J(B)
as a direct factor of
the algebra
A/J(A)
in a unique way.

Passing to the dual coalgebra, one obtains what can be called
“the structure theory of finite-dimensional coassociative
coalgebras”
E
.
Any such coalgebra has a unique maximal cosemisimple subcoalgebra

E^{\mathrm{ss}}
, which decomposes uniquely as a direct sum of cosimple
coalgebras.
The (noncounital) quotient coalgebra
E/E^{\mathrm{ss}}
is conilpotent.

Moreover, if
E\subset D
is a subcoalgebra in a finite-dimensional
coalgebra, then
E^{\mathrm{ss}}=E\cap D^{\mathrm{ss}}
.
The cosemisimple coalgebra
D^{\mathrm{ss}}
can be decomposed as a direct
sum of its subcoalgebra
E^{\mathrm{ss}}
and a complementary subcoalgebra in
a unique way.

Let us formulate the general definition of a
cosemisimple
coalgebra
, in the form of a list of equivalent conditions in
the next lemma.
Here a nonzero coassociative, counital coalgebra over
k
is called

cosimple
if it has no nonzero proper subcoalgebras.

Lemma 3.3
.

For any coassociative, counital coalgebra
C
over a field
k
,
the following conditions are equivalent:

(1)

the abelian category of left
C
-comodules is semisimple;

(2)

the abelian category of right
C
-comodules is semisimple;

(3)

C
is the sum of its cosimple subcoalgebras;

(4)

C
uniquely decomposes as a direct sum of its
(finite-dimensional) cosimple subcoalgebras, with all the cosimple
subcoalgebras of
C
appearing as direct summands in this direct sum
decomposition. ∎

Now let
C
be an arbitrary (infinite-dimensional) coassociative
coalgebra.
Representing
C
as the directed union of its finite-dimensional
subcoalgebras
E
and passing to the direct limit, one obtains
the following structural result.

Proposition 3.4
.

Any coassociative, counital coalgebra
C
over a field
k
contains
a unique maximal cosemisimple subcoalgebra
C^{\mathrm{ss}}
, which is can be
obtained as the sum of all co(semi)simple subcoalgebras of
C
.
Simultaneously,
C^{\mathrm{ss}}
is the unique minimal subcoalgebra among
all the subcoalgebras
D\subset C
for which that the (noncounital)
quotient coalgebra
C/D
is conilpotent. ∎

Following the discussion in
Section
3.3
, the conilpotent
noncounital coalgebra
D=C/C^{\mathrm{ss}}
is endowed with a natural increasing
filtration
F
.
Denoting by
F_{n}C
the full preimage of
F_{n}(C/C^{\mathrm{ss}})
under
the natural surjective homomorphism
C\longrightarrow C/C^{\mathrm{ss}}
, one obtains
a natural increasing filtration
F
on
C
, called the
coradical
filtration
.
What we call the “maximal cosemisimple subcoalgebra”
C^{\mathrm{ss}}
of
a coalgebra
C
is otherwise known as the
coradical
of
C

[
88
, Chapters VIII–IX]
,
[
49
, Chapter 5]
.

Remark 3.5
.

The definitions of the coradical filtration in
[
88
,
49
]
are
formulated in terms of the so-called
wedge
operation on
vector subspaces of a coalgebra.
Let
C
be a coassociative coalgebra over
k
and
X
,
Y\subset C

be two vector subspaces.
According to
[
88
, Section 9.0]
,
[
49
, proof of
Theorem 5.2.2]
, the wedge
X\wedge Y\subset C
is defined as
the kernel of the composition
C\longrightarrow C\otimes_{k}C\longrightarrow C/X\otimes_{k}C/Y

of the comultiplication map
C\longrightarrow C\otimes_{k}C
with the natural
surjection
C\otimes_{k}C\longrightarrow C/X\otimes_{k}C/Y
.
According to
[
88
, Proposition 9.0.0(c)]
, the wedge operation
is associative.
By the definition, put
\bigwedge^{1}X=X
and

\bigwedge^{n}X=(\bigwedge^{n-1}X)\wedge X
for
n\geq 2
.

Notice that the definition of the wedge makes perfect sense for
noncounital coalgebras
D
as well.
Arguing by induction on
n
, one easily shows that
\bigwedge^{n}X\subset D
is the kernel of the composition
D\longrightarrow D^{\otimes n}\longrightarrow(D/X)^{\otimes n}
of the iterated comultiplication map

D\longrightarrow D^{\otimes n}
with the natural surjection
D^{\otimes n}\longrightarrow(D/X)^{\otimes n}
(for every
n\geq 1
).
Then, for a noncounital conilpotent coalgebra
D
, the definition
of the natural increasing filtration
F
on
D
given in
Section
3.3
can be rephrased
as
F_{n}D=\bigwedge^{n+1}0\subset D
.

Let
p\colon C\longrightarrow D
be a homomorphism of noncounital coalgebras.
Then one can easily see that the wedge operation commutes with
taking the full preimages of vector subspaces under
p
: for any
subspaces
X
,
Y\subset D
one has

p^{-1}(X\wedge Y)=p^{-1}X\wedge p^{-1}Y
, hence

p^{-1}(\bigwedge^{n}X)=\bigwedge^{n}(p^{-1}X)
.

Now let
C
be a coassociative, counital coalgebra over
k
.
The coradical filtration is defined in
[
88
, Section 9.1]
by
the rule
C_{n}=\bigwedge^{n+1}C^{\mathrm{ss}}\subset C
, where
C^{\mathrm{ss}}\subset C

is what we call the maximal cosemisimple subcoalgebra (called
the coradical in
[
88
,
49
]
).
In the book
[
49
, §5.2]
, the coradical filtration is defined by
induction using the rules
C_{0}=C^{\mathrm{ss}}
and, essentially,

C_{n}=C^{\mathrm{ss}}\wedge C_{n-1}\subset C
; this is clearly equivalent to
the definition in
[
88
]
.
Taking
D=C/C^{\mathrm{ss}}
, one concludes that
C_{n}=\bigwedge^{n+1}C^{\mathrm{ss}}=\bigwedge^{n+1}p^{-1}(0)=p^{-1}(\bigwedge^{n+1}0)=p^{-1}(F_{n}D)=F_{n}C
(where
p\colon C\longrightarrow D
is the natural surjection).
Thus the definition of the coradical filtration by the rule

C_{n}=\bigwedge^{n+1}C^{\mathrm{ss}}
in
[
88
,
49
]
agrees with our definition
of it by the rule
F_{n}C=p^{-1}(F_{n}D)
given above in this section.

A counital coalgebra
C
is conilpotent (in the sense of
the definition in Section
3.3
) if
and only if there is an isomorphism of coalgebras
C^{\mathrm{ss}}\simeq k
,
or in other words, if and only if the coalgebra
C^{\mathrm{ss}}

is one-dimensional.

4.
DG-Algebras and DG-Coalgebras

The aim of this section is to formulate a simple version of
Koszul duality between algebras and coalgebras (rather than modules
and comodules).
The notion of a
filtered quasi-isomorphism
of
DG-coalgebras
[
30
]
plays a key role.

4.1.
Augmented DG-algebras

Let
A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}=(A,d)
be a DG-algebra over a field
k
.
So
A=\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}A^{i}
is a graded
k
-algebra and

d\colon A\longrightarrow A
is a homogeneous
k
-linear map of degree
1

satisfying the Leibniz rule with signs with respect to
the multiplication (in other words,
d
is an odd derivation of
A
)
such that
d^{2}=0
.

An
augmentation

\alpha
on
A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
is a (unital) DG-algebra
homomorphism
\alpha\colon A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\longrightarrow k
.
So
\alpha(a)=0
for all
a\in A^{i}
,
i\neq 0
,
\alpha(ab)=\alpha(a)\alpha(b)
for all
a
,
b\in A
,
\alpha(1)=1
, and

\alpha(d(a))=0
for all
a\in A^{-1}
.
We denote by
A^{{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}},+}=\ker(\alpha)\subset A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
the augmentation
ideal.
So
A^{{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}},+}
is a homogeneous two-sided DG-ideal in
A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
, and

A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}=k\oplus A^{{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}},+}
as a complex of
k
-vector spaces.

The
bar-construction

\Br^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\alpha}(A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})
of an augmented
DG-algebra
(A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}},\alpha)
is the bicomplex

k\overset{0}{\mskip 1.5mu\leftarrow\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\relbar\mskip 1.5mu}A^{{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}},+}\mskip 1.5mu\leftarrow\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\relbar\mskip 1.5muA^{{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}},+}\otimes_{k}A^{{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}},+}\mskip 1.5mu\leftarrow\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\relbar\mskip 1.5muA^{{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}},+}\otimes_{k}A^{{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}},+}\otimes_{k}A^{{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}},+}\mskip 1.5mu\leftarrow\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\relbar\mskip 1.5mu\dotsb

with the bar differential
\partial
given by the formulas similar to those
in Section
1.1
(except for the
\pm
signs,
which are more complicated in the case of a DG-algebra
A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
) and
the differential
d
induced by the differential
d
on
A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
.
The differential
d
on
\Br^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\alpha}(A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})
acts on every
tensor power
A^{{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}},+}{}^{\otimes n}
of the augmentation ideal

A^{{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}},+}
by the tensor product of
n
copies of the differential
d

on
A^{{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}},+}
(in the sense of the tensor product of complexes).

The total complex of the bar-construction
\Br^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\alpha}(A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})

is produced by taking infinite direct sums along the diagonals.
The underlying graded vector space of
\Br^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\alpha}(A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})
is
endowed with the graded coalgebra structure of the tensor coalgebra

\Br_{\alpha}(A)=\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$T$}(A^{+}[1])
.
Here the shift of cohomological grading
[1]
in the latter formula is
a way to express the passage to the total complex of the bicomplex

\Br^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\alpha}(A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})
, with its total grading equal to the grading
induced by the grading of
A
minus the grading by the number of
the tensor factors.
This makes
\Br^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\alpha}(A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})
a DG-coalgebra (as defined in
Section
2.2
).
We refer to
[
64
, Section 6.1]
for further details, incuding
the (not so trivial) sign rules for the differentials
\partial
and
d
.

4.2.
Coaugmented DG-coalgebras

Let
C^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}=(C,d)
be a DG-coalgebra over
k

(see Section
2.2
).
A
coaugmentation

\gamma
on
C^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
is a (counital)
DG-coalgebra homomorphism
\gamma\colon k\longrightarrow C
.
So
\gamma(k)\subset C^{0}
,
\gamma
is a homomorphism of coalgebras,
the composition
k\overset{\gamma}{\longrightarrow}C\overset{\epsilon}{\longrightarrow}k
is
the identity map, and
d(\gamma(1))=0
.
We put
C^{{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}},+}=\coker(\gamma)
; so
C^{+}
is, generally speaking,
a DG-coalgebra without counit, and
C^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}=k\oplus C^{{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}},+}
as
a complex of
k
-vector spaces.

The
cobar-construction

\Cb^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\gamma}(C^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})
of
a coaugmented DG-coalgebra
(C^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}},\gamma)
is the bicomplex

k\overset{0}{\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5mu}C^{{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}},+}\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5muC^{{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}},+}\otimes_{k}C^{{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}},+}\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5muC^{{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}},+}\otimes_{k}C^{{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}},+}\otimes_{k}C^{{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}},+}\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5mu\dotsb

with the cobar differential
\partial
given by the formulas similar to
those in Section
2.6
(except for
the
\pm
signs) and the differential
d
induced by
the differential
d
on
C^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
.
The differential
d
on
\Cb^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\gamma}(C^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})
acts on every
tensor power
C^{{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}},+}{}^{\otimes n}
of the complex
C^{{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}},+}
by
the tensor product of
n
copies of the differential
d
on
C^{{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}},+}
.

The total complex of the cobar-construction
\Cb^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\gamma}(C^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})

is produced by taking infinite direct sums along the diagonals.
The underlying graded vector space of
\Cb^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\gamma}(C^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})
is
endowed with the graded algebra structure of the tensor algebra

\Cb_{\gamma}(C)=T(C^{+}[-1])
.
Here the shift of cohomological grading
[-1]
is a way to express
the passage to the total complex of the bicomplex

\Cb^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\gamma}(C^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})
, with its total grading equal to the grading
induced by the grading of
C
plus the grading by the number of
tensor factors.
This makes
\Cb^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\gamma}(C^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})
a DG-algebra.
We refer to
[
64
, Section 6.1]
for further details,
including the sign rules for the differentials
\partial
and
d
.

4.3.
Conilpotent DG-coalgebras

A noncounital graded coalgebra
D
is called
conilpotent
if its
underlying noncounital ungraded coalgebra is conilpotent (see
the definition in Section
3.3
).
A coaugmented graded coalgebra
(C,\gamma)
is called

conilpotent
if its underlying coaugmented ungraded coalgebra
is conilpotent; equivalently, this means that the noncounital
graded coalgebra
D=C/\gamma(k)
is conilpotent.

A coaugmented DG-coalgebra
(C^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}},\gamma)
is called

conilpotent
if its underlying graded coalgebra
C
is conilpotent.
In other words, a DG-coalgebra
C^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
is conilpotent if and only if
the graded coalgebra
C
is conilpotent
and
its coaugmentation

\gamma\colon k\longrightarrow C
is a DG-coalgebra map, that is,
d(\gamma(1))=0
.

By construction, the canonical increasing filtration
F
on a conilpotent
(noncounital or coaugmented) graded coalgebra is a filtration by
homogeneous vector subspaces.
The canonical increasing filtration
F
on a conilpotent DG-coalgebra

C^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
is a filtration by subcomplexes
