The curvature linear function
h\colon\Br^{-2}(A)\longrightarrow k
also has two
possibly nonzero bigrading components, namely, the maps

m_{k}\colon(A_{+}\otimes_{k}A_{+})^{0}\longrightarrow k
and
d_{k}\colon A_{+}^{-1}\longrightarrow k
.
We refer to
[
64
, Section 6.1]
for the sign rules.

Now let
v^{\prime}\colon A\longrightarrow k
be another homogeneous
k
-linear map
such that
v^{\prime}(1)=1
.
Then the same construction as above produces a CDG-coalgebra
structure
\Br^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}_{v^{\prime}}(A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})\allowbreak=(\Br(A),d^{\prime},h^{\prime})
on
the graded coalgebra
\Br(A)
.
Similarly to the discussion in
Section
6.4
,
the CDG-coalgebras
\Br^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}_{v}(A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})
and
\Br^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}_{v^{\prime}}(A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})

are connected by a natural change-of-connection isomorphism

(\mathrm{id},l)\colon\Br^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}_{v}(A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})\longrightarrow\Br^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}_{v^{\prime}}(A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})
.
Here
l\colon\Br^{-1}(A)\longrightarrow k
is a change-of-connection linear
function whose only nonzero bigrading component is the linear function

l\colon A_{+}^{0}\longrightarrow k
measuring the difference between the retractions

v
and
v^{\prime}
.

Recall the notation
k{\operatorname{\mathsf{--alg}}}_{\mathsf{dg}}
introduced in
Section
4.4
for
the category of DG-algebras over
k
.
Denote by
k{\operatorname{\mathsf{--alg}}}_{\mathsf{dg}}^{+}\subset k{\operatorname{\mathsf{--alg}}}_{\mathsf{dg}}
the full subcategory of

nonzero
DG-algebras; and let
k{\operatorname{\mathsf{--coalg}}}_{\mathsf{cdg}}
denote
the category of CDG-coalgebras over
k
.

Proposition 6.1
.

The assignment of the curved DG-coalgebra
\Br^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}_{v}(A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})
to
a DG-algebra
A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
can be naturally extended to a functor from
the category of nonzero DG-algebras to the category of
CDG-coalgebras over
k
,

\Br^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}_{v}\colon k{\operatorname{\mathsf{--alg}}}_{\mathsf{dg}}^{+}\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5muk{\operatorname{\mathsf{--coalg}}}_{\mathsf{cdg}}.

Proof.

In order to construct the functor
\Br^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}_{v}
, one has to choose
for every nonzero DG-algebra
A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
over
k
a homogeneous

k
-linear retraction onto its unit
v_{A}\colon A\longrightarrow k
.
Such retractions can be chosen in an arbitrary way, and do not need
agree with each other in any sense.
The resulting functor
\Br^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}_{v}
, viewed up to a uniquely defined
isomorphism of functors, does not depend on the choice of
the retractions
v_{A}
.

Given a homomorphism of DG-algebras
f\colon A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\longrightarrow B^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
, each
of them endowed with a homogeneous
k
-linear retraction onto its unit

v_{A}\colon A\longrightarrow k
and
v_{B}\colon B\longrightarrow k
, one constructs the induced
morphism of CDG-coalgebras
(g,l)\colon\Br^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}_{v_{A}}(A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})\longrightarrow\Br^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}_{v_{B}}(B^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})
in the way similar to the one in
Section
6.4
.
The map
f
induces a homogeneous
k
-linear map
f_{+}\colon A_{+}\longrightarrow B_{+}
of degree
0
, which in turn induces a graded coalgebra
homomorphism
g\colon\Br(A)=\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$T$}(A_{+}[1])\longrightarrow\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$T$}(B_{+}[1])=\Br(B)
.
The only nonzero bigrading component of the change-of-connection
linear function
l\colon\Br^{-1}(A)\longrightarrow k
is the linear function

l\colon A_{+}^{0}\longrightarrow k
measuring the difference between the retractions

v_{A}
and
v_{B}\circ f\colon A\longrightarrow k
.
∎

6.6.
Curved, noncoaugmented cobar-construction

This section is a generalization of
Section
4.2
to curved,
noncoaugmented DG-coalgebras.
The constructions below are dual to those of
Section
6.5
, up to a point.
But then there are subtle differences between two dual pictures,
which we will explain.

Let
C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}=(C,d_{C},h_{C})
be a nonzero CDG-coalgebra over a field
k
.
Then the counit
\epsilon\colon C\longrightarrow k
is a nonzero homogeneous
linear map of degree
0
.
Consider the graded vector subspace
C_{+}=\ker(\epsilon)\subset C
.

Choose a homogeneous
k
-linear map
w\colon k\longrightarrow C
of degree
0

such that the composition
k\overset{w}{\longrightarrow}C\overset{\epsilon}{\longrightarrow}k
is the identity map.
So
w
is a homogeneous
k
-linear section of the counit
map
\epsilon
.
Put
W=\coker(w)
; then the composition
C_{+}\longrightarrow C\longrightarrow W

is an isomorphism of graded
k
-vector spaces and
C=k\oplus W

as a graded
k
-vector space.

Both the comultiplication map
\mu\colon C\longrightarrow C\otimes_{k}C
and
the differential
d_{C}\colon C\longrightarrow C
decompose into components according
to the direct sum decomposition
C=k\oplus W
.
The projections of
\mu
onto
k\cdot 1\otimes_{k}C
and
C\otimes_{k}k\cdot 1

are prescribed by the counitality axiom, while the composition of
d_{C}

with the counit map vanishes; so we do not need to keep track of these.
The composition of the map
\mu
with the projection
C\otimes_{k}C\longrightarrow W\otimes_{k}W
provides a
k
-linear map
k\oplus W=C\longrightarrow W\otimes_{k}W
,
whose components we denote by
\mu_{W}\colon W\longrightarrow W\otimes_{k}W
and

\mu_{k}\colon k\longrightarrow W\otimes_{k}W
.
The composition of the map
d_{C}
with the projection
C\longrightarrow W

provides a
k
-linear map
k\oplus W=C\longrightarrow W
, whose components
we denote by
d_{W}\colon W\longrightarrow W
and
d_{k}\colon k\longrightarrow W
.

Denote by
\Cb(C)
the free graded algebra
T(C_{+}[-1])
.
One observes that, for any graded
k
-vector space
V
, odd
derivations
d
of degree
1
on the tensor algebra
T(V)
are
uniquely determined by their restructions to the subspace
(direct summand) of generators,
V=V^{\otimes 1}\subset T(V)
.
Moreover, any homogeneous linear map
V\longrightarrow T(V)
of degree
1

corresponds to some odd derivation
d
on
T(V)
in this way.

We are going to construct a CDG-algebra
\Cb^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}_{w}(C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}})=(\Cb(C),d_{\Cb},h_{\Cb})
, which can be called the curved,
noncoaugmented cobar-construction of a CDG-coalgebra
C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
.
Specifically, the odd derivation
d_{\Cb}\colon\Cb(C)\longrightarrow\Cb(C)
of
degree
1
is defined by the condition that its restriction to
the subspace of generators
C_{+}\subset\Cb(C)
has three possibly
nonzero bigrading components, given by the maps
\mu_{W}\colon C_{+}\longrightarrow C_{+}\otimes_{k}C_{+}
and
d_{W}\colon C_{+}\longrightarrow C_{+}
,
and
also by
the curvature linear function
h_{C}\colon C_{+}^{-2}=C^{-2}\longrightarrow k
.
The curvature element
h_{\Cb}\in\Cb^{2}(C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}})
has two possibly
nonzero bigrading components, namely, the elements
\mu_{k}(1)\in(C_{+}\otimes_{k}\penalty C_{+})^{0}
and
d_{k}(1)\in C_{+}^{1}=C^{1}
.
The sign rules can be found in
[
64
, Section 6.1]
.

Remark 6.2
.

The first subtlety which deserves to be mentioned is that the previous
paragraph presumes that degree
-2
is different from degree
0
.
This is the case with the usual gradings by the group of integers,
which are generally presumed in this paper.
But sometimes one may be interested in
2
-periodic complexes,
DG-algebras, CDG-coalgebras etc., graded by the group
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}

(or by some other grading group, as in
[
64
, Remark 1.1]

and
[
59
, Section 1.1]
).
When
2=0
in the grading group
\Gamma
, the equality
C_{+}^{-2}=C^{-2}
no longer holds, and the curvature linear function
h_{C}

also decomposes into two components,
h_{W}\colon W\longrightarrow k
and

h_{k}\colon k\longrightarrow k
.
In this case,
h_{W}
becomes a part of the differential
d_{\Cb}
,
and
h_{k}
becomes a third component of the curvature element
h_{\Cb}

in the cobar-construction (see
[
64
, Section 6.1]
).

One can go further and consider the grading group
\Gamma=\{0\}
,
which makes sense over a ground field
k
of characteristic
2

(otherwise there are sign issues forcing the complexes to be at
least
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded).
In this setting, even change-of-connection elements/linear functions
can have nonzero unit/counit components (as
1=0
in
\Gamma
).
We refer to
[
65
, Section 6.1]
for a discussion of the bar-
and cobar-constructions including a treatment of these unconventional
grading effects special to characteristic
2
.

A
coaugmentation

\gamma
on a CDG-coalgebra
(C,d,h)

is a morphism of (graded, counital) coalgebras
\gamma\colon k\longrightarrow C

such that
(\gamma,0)\colon(k,0,0)\longrightarrow(C,d,h)
is a morphism of
CDG-coalgebras.
This definition includes the condition that the composition

k\overset{\gamma}{\longrightarrow}C\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}C
vanishes.
(When
2=0
in the grading group
\Gamma
, this definition also
includes the condition that the composition
k\overset{\gamma}{\longrightarrow}C\overset{h}{\longrightarrow}k
vanishes.)

Given a coaugmented CDG-coalgebra
(C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}},\gamma)
, one can take
the section
w=\gamma
in the construction above and produce
a
DG-algebra

\Cb^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\gamma}(C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}})=(\Cb(C),d_{\Cb})
.
By the definition, the maps
\mu_{k}
and
d_{k}
vanish in this case,
so
h_{\Cb}=0
.

Otherwise, when no natural coaugmentation is available for
C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
,
one has to choose an arbitrary section
w\colon k\longrightarrow C
to apply
the cobar-construction, and the issue of changing the section arises.
Let
w^{\prime}\colon k\longrightarrow C
be another homogeneous
k
-linear map
of degree
0
such that
\epsilon\circ w^{\prime}=\mathrm{id}_{k}
.
Then the difference
b=w^{\prime}(1)-w(1)
is an element of the vector
subspace
C_{+}^{0}\subset C
.
The vector space
C_{+}^{0}
is one of the bigrading components of
the vector space
\Cb^{1}(C)
; so
b\in C_{+}^{0}\subset\Cb^{1}(C)
.
The CDG-algebras
\Cb^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}_{w}(C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}})
and
\Cb^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}_{w^{\prime}}(C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}})
are
connected by a natural change-of-connection isomorphism

(\mathrm{id}_{\Cb},b)\colon\Cb^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}_{w^{\prime}}(C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}})\longrightarrow\Cb^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}_{w}(C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}})
.

Now suppose that we are given a change-of-connection isomorphism
of CDG-coalgebras
(\mathrm{id},a)\colon(C,d,h)\longrightarrow(C,d^{\prime},h^{\prime})
, where

a\colon C^{-1}\longrightarrow k
is a change-of-connection linear function
on
C
.
Let
w\colon k\longrightarrow C
be a homogeneous
k
-linear section of the counit.
Then the two cobar-constructions
\Cb^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}_{w}(C,d,h)
and

\Cb^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}_{w}(C,d^{\prime},h^{\prime})
are naturally isomorphic as CDG-algebras.

To construct the latter isomorphism, one only needs to follow
the philosophy of nonaugmented Koszul duality, which tells that
change-of-connection isomorphisms on one side correspond to
changes of the chosen retraction of the unit or section of
the counit on the other side.
The cobar-construction
\Cb^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}_{w}(C,d^{\prime},h^{\prime})
comes endowed with
a natural retraction onto the unit
v_{\Cb}
, namely, the direct
summand projection
\Cb(C)\longrightarrow C_{+}^{\otimes 0}=k
.
(The map
v_{\Cb}
is even an augmentation of the graded algebra

\Cb(C)
, but it is
not
an augmentation of the CDG-algebra

\Cb^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}_{w}(C,d,h)
unless
h_{C}=0
.)
The desired isomorphism
\Cb^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}_{w}(C,d,h)\simeq\Cb^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}_{w}(C,d^{\prime},h^{\prime})

is supposed
not
to preserve these retractions onto the unit.

Coming to the point, given two graded vector spaces
V
and
W
,
any homogeneous
k
-linear map
V\longrightarrow T(W)
of degree
0
can be
uniquely extended to a graded
k
-algebra homomorphism

T(V)\longrightarrow T(W)
.
Consider the graded
k
-algebra automorphism
f_{a}\colon\Cb(C)\longrightarrow\Cb(C)
defined by the rule
c\longmapsto c+a(c)
for all

c\in C_{+}=V=W
.
Here
c+a(c)
is an element of the graded vector space
V\oplus k=V^{\otimes 1}\oplus V^{\otimes 0}\subset T(V)=\Cb(C)
.
Then we have a natural isomorphism of CDG-coalgebras

(f_{a},0)\colon\Cb^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}_{w}(C,d^{\prime},h^{\prime})\longrightarrow\Cb^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}_{w}(C,d,h)
.

Recall the notation
k{\operatorname{\mathsf{--coalg}}}_{\mathsf{cdg}}
introduced in
Section
6.5
for
the category of CDG-coalgebras over
k
.
Denote by
k{\operatorname{\mathsf{--coalg}}}_{\mathsf{cdg}}^{+}\subset k{\operatorname{\mathsf{--coalg}}}_{\mathsf{cdg}}
the full subcategory
of nonzero CDG-coalgebras, and by
k{\operatorname{\mathsf{--coalg}}}_{\mathsf{cdg}}^{\mathsf{coaug}}

the category of coaugmented CDG-coalgebras.
Furthermore, let
k{\operatorname{\mathsf{--alg}}}_{\mathsf{cdg}}
denote the category of CDG-algebras
over
k
.

Proposition 6.3
.

(a)
The assignment of the DG-algebra
\Cb^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\gamma}(C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}})

to a coaugmented CDG-coalgebra
(C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}},\gamma)
can be naturally
extended to a functor from the category of coaugmented CDG-coalgebras
to the category of DG-algebras over
k
,

\Cb^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\gamma}\colon k{\operatorname{\mathsf{--coalg}}}_{\mathsf{cdg}}^{\mathsf{coaug}}\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5muk{\operatorname{\mathsf{--alg}}}_{\mathsf{dg}}.

(b)
The assignment of the CDG-algebra
\Cb^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}_{w}(C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}})

to a CDG-coalgebra
C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
can be naturally extended to a functor
from the category of nonzero CDG-coalgebras to the category of
CDG-algebras over
k
,

\Cb^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}_{w}\colon k{\operatorname{\mathsf{--coalg}}}_{\mathsf{cdg}}^{+}\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5muk{\operatorname{\mathsf{--alg}}}_{\mathsf{cdg}}.

Proof.

Part (a): let
C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}=(C,d_{C},h_{C})
and
D^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}=(D,d_{D},h_{D})
be two
CDG-coalgebras endowed with coaugmentations
\gamma\colon k\longrightarrow C

and
\delta\colon k\longrightarrow D
.
Let
(f,a)\colon(C,\gamma)\longrightarrow(D,\delta)
be a morphism of coaugmented
CDG-coalgebras, i. e., a morphism of CDG-coalgebras preserving
coaugmentations, in the sense that
f\circ\gamma=\delta
.
(When
1=0
in the grading group
\Gamma
, the condition that

a\circ\gamma=0
should be added.)

The morphism of DG-algebras
g\colon\Cb^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\gamma}(C)\longrightarrow\Cb^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\delta}(D)
induced by the coaugmented CDG-coalgebra
morphism
(f,a)
is constructed as follows.
The map
f
induces a homogeneous
k
-linear map
f_{+}\colon C_{+}\longrightarrow D_{+}
of degree
0
.
The graded
k
-algebra homomorphism
g\colon\Cb(C)=T(C_{+}[-1])\longrightarrow T(D_{+}[-1])=\Cb(D)
is defined by the rule

c\longmapsto f_{+}(c)-a(c)
for all
c\in C_{+}=V
.
Here
f_{+}(c)-a(c)
is an element of the graded vector space

W\oplus k=W^{\otimes 1}\oplus W^{\otimes 0}\subset T(W)=\Cb(D)
,
where
W=D_{+}
.

To construct the functor in part (b), one has to choose for every
nonzero CDG-coalgebra
C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
over
k
a homogeneous
k
-linear
section of its counit
w_{C}\colon k\longrightarrow C
.
Such sections are chosen in an arbitrary way, and do not need to
agree with each other in any sense.
The resulting functor
\Cb^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}_{w}
, viewed up to a uniquely defined
isomorphism of functors, does not depend on the choice of
the sections
w_{C}
.

The construction of the morphism of CDG-algebras
(g,b)\colon\Cb_{w_{C}}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}(C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}})\longrightarrow\Cb_{w_{D}}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}(D^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}})
assigned to a morphism
of CDG-coalgebras
(f,a)\colon C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}\longrightarrow D^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
by the functor

\Cb^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}_{w}
in part (b) combines the construction from the proof of
part (a) with the dual version of the construction from the proof of
Proposition
6.1
.
We skip further details, which are rather straightforward:
the section/retraction and connection changes are taken care of as
described in the discussion preceding the proposition.
∎

Remark 6.4
.

One conspicuous difference between the expositions in
Sections
6.5

and
6.6
is that
the bar-construction was only applied to
uncurved
DG-algebras

A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}=(A,d)
in Section
6.5
,
while the cobar-construction was defined for
curved

CDG-coalgebras
C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}=(C,d,h)
in
Section
6.6
.
Let us explain the situation.

Actually, one can define the bar-construction in the context of
curved DG-algebras and assign a CDG-coalgebra

\Br^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}_{v}(B^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}})=(\Br(B),d_{\Br},h_{\Br})
to any CDG-algebra

B^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}=(B,d_{B},h_{B})
with a chosen retraction onto the unit line

v\colon B\longrightarrow k
.
When the CDG-algebra
B^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
is augmented and

\beta\colon B^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}\longrightarrow k
is the augmentation, the construction
produces a DG-coalgebra
\Br^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\beta}(B^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}})=(\Br(B),d_{\Br})

(as
h_{\Br}=0
in this case).

The latter approach was suggested in the paper
[
54
]
.
Subsequently it was discovered and reported in
the paper
[
44
, Section 5.1]
that the approach does not work
as intended.

In fact, there is a massive loss of information involved with
the passage from an (augmented or nonaugmented) CDG-algebra

B^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}=(B,d_{B},h_{B})
over a field
k
with a nonzero curvature
element
h_{B}\neq 0
to the bar-construction
\Br^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}_{v}(B^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}})
.
In particular,
all (C)DG-comodules over the (C)DG-coalgebra

\Br^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}_{v}(B^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}})
are contractible
when
h_{B}\neq 0
.
This vanishing phenomenon, originally observed by Kontsevich, was
recorded in the present author’s memoir
[
64
, Remark 7.3]
.

The Kontsevich vanishing can be avoided by working with so-called

weakly curved
DG-algebras, which presumes a more complicated
setting with a
topological local ring of coefficients
instead
of a ground field
k
.
Then the theory becomes well-behaved, as it was established
in the memoir
[
65
]
, where the Koszul duality theory for weakly
curved DG-algebras and weakly curved
\mathrm{A}_{\infty}
-algebras
was developed.

Returning to the construction of the CDG-coalgebra
\Br^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}_{v}(B^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}})

over a field
k
, the reader can find some details of this
(well-defined, but not well-behaved) construction
in
[
64
, Section 6.1]
, with a further discussion
in
[
64
, Remark 6.1]
.
One point is worth mentioning: even though one can construct
the CDG-coalgebra
\Br^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}_{v}(B^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}})
or even the DG-coalgebra

\Br^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\beta}(B^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}})
, one
cannot
assign
a (C)DG-coalgebra isomorphism to a change-of-connection
isomorphism of CDG-algebras
(\mathrm{id}_{B},a)\colon(B,d^{\prime},h^{\prime})\longrightarrow(B,d,h)

with a change-of-connection element
0\neq a\in B^{1}
.

The explanation is that the tensor coalgebra
\Br(B)=\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$T$}(B_{+})
is
conilpotent, hence uniquely coaugmented; and while there are
coderivations of the tensor coalgebras
\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$T$}(W)
which do not
preserve the coaugmentations, there exist
no
such endomorphisms
or homomorphisms of tensor coalgebras.
A useful intuition may be provided by the construction of the dual
algebra
C^{*}
to a coalgebra
C
, as mentioned in
Sections
3.2

and
6.3
.
Suppose that
W
is a finite-dimensional (ungraded)
k
-vector
space, and consider the tensor coalgebra
C=\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$T$}(W)
.
Then the dual algebra
C^{*}
is the algebra of noncommutative

formal Taylor power series
in a finite number of variables
over
k
.
On the other hand,
T(W)
is the algebra of noncommutative

polynomials
.
Now, variable changes like
z\longmapsto z+1
are well-defined as
automorphisms of (commutative or noncommutative) polynomial rings,
but one cannot make such a variable change act on the ring of formal
power series in
z
.
Still,
\partial/\partial z_{i}
are well-defined derivations of the formal power
series ring
k[[z_{1},\dotsc,z_{n}]]
(or its noncommutative version);
they just cannot be integrated/exponentiated to automorphisms.

To sum up the discussion in this remark, one can say that
coderivations of the tensor coalgebras
\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$T$}(W)
which do not preserve
the coaugmentation may be well-defined, but they are
not

well-behaved in the context of differential graded homological algebra.
This is one difference between the properties of the tensor algebras
and the tensor coalgebras; this is also an explanation of
the Kontsevich vanishing.

The problem of nonfunctoriality of the bar-construction with respect
to change-of-connection morphisms of CDG-algebras is resolved by
passing from the usual (conilpotent) to the “extended”
(nonconilpotent) bar-construction of
[
28
, Definition 2.5]
.
See the discussion in
[
28
, Section 4]
.

6.7.
Duality between DG-algebras and CDG-coalgebras

Let
C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}=(C,d,h)
be a CDG-coalgebra and
\gamma\colon k\longrightarrow C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}

be a coaugmentation of
C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
(as defined in
Section
6.6
).
The CDG-coalgebra
C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
is said to be
conilpotent
if
the coaugmented graded coalgebra
(C,\gamma)
is conilpotent.
In other words, a CDG-coalgebra
C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
is conilpotent if and only if
the graded coalgebra
C
is conilpotent with the (unique)
coaugmentation
\gamma

and

(\gamma,0)\colon k\longrightarrow C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}

is a morphism of CDG-coalgebras.

As above, we denote by
k{\operatorname{\mathsf{--alg}}}_{\mathsf{dg}}
the category of DG-algebras
over
k
and by
k{\operatorname{\mathsf{--alg}}}_{\mathsf{dg}}^{+}\subset k{\operatorname{\mathsf{--alg}}}_{\mathsf{dg}}
the full subcategory
of nonzero DG-algebras.
Furthermore,
k{\operatorname{\mathsf{--coalg}}}_{\mathsf{cdg}}
is the category of CDG-coalgebras
over
k
, and
k{\operatorname{\mathsf{--coalg}}}_{\mathsf{cdg}}^{\mathsf{coaug}}
is the category of coaugmented
CDG-coalgebras (with the CDG-coalgebra morphisms
(f,a)\colon C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}\longrightarrow D^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
forming a commutative triangle with the coaugmentations

(\gamma,0)\colon k\longrightarrow C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
and
(\delta,0)\colon k\longrightarrow D^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
).
Denote by
k{\operatorname{\mathsf{--coalg}}}_{\mathsf{cdg}}^{\mathsf{conilp}}\subset k{\operatorname{\mathsf{--coalg}}}_{\mathsf{cdg}}^{\mathsf{coaug}}

the full subcategory of conilpotent CDG-coalgebras.

Somewhat similarly to the discussion in
Section
4.4
,
the bar-construction
\Br^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}_{v}(A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})
of any DG-algebra
A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}

is naturally a coaugmented CDG-coalgebra (with the direct summand
inclusion
k=A_{+}^{\otimes 0}\longrightarrow\Br(A)
providing the coaugmentation).
Moreover, the coaugmented CDG-coalgebra
\Br^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}_{v}(A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})
is
conilpotent.

So the bar-construction of
Proposition
6.1

is actually a functor

\Br^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}_{v}\colon k{\operatorname{\mathsf{--alg}}}_{\mathsf{dg}}^{+}\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5muk{\operatorname{\mathsf{--coalg}}}_{\mathsf{cdg}}^{\mathsf{conilp}}.

The curved coaugmented cobar-construction of
Proposition
6.3
(a)
obviously produces nonzero DG-algebras, so is a functor

\Cb^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\gamma}\colon k{\operatorname{\mathsf{--coalg}}}_{\mathsf{cdg}}^{\mathsf{coaug}}\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5muk{\operatorname{\mathsf{--alg}}}_{\mathsf{dg}}^{+}.

Lemma 6.5
.

The restriction of the cobar-construction to conilpotent
CDG-coalgebras,

\Cb^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\gamma}\colon k{\operatorname{\mathsf{--coalg}}}_{\mathsf{cdg}}^{\mathsf{conilp}}\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5muk{\operatorname{\mathsf{--alg}}}_{\mathsf{dg}}^{+},

is naturally a left adjoint functor to the bar-construction

\Br^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}_{v}\colon k{\operatorname{\mathsf{--alg}}}_{\mathsf{dg}}^{+}\longrightarrow k{\operatorname{\mathsf{--coalg}}}_{\mathsf{cdg}}^{\mathsf{conilp}}
.

Lemma
6.5
is
a curved/nonaugmented version of
Lemma
4.1
.
A very brief hint of proof will be given in
Section
6.8
below.

One would like to define equivalence relations (that is, the classes
of morphisms to be inverted) in the categories of (nonzero)
DG-algebras and conilpotent CDG-coalgebras so that the adjoint
functors
\Br^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}_{v}
and
\Cb^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\gamma}
become equivalences of
categories after these classes of morphisms are inverted.
In the case of DG-algebras, one can use the conventional
quasi-isomorphisms; but the notion of a quasi-isomorphism of
CDG-coalgebras is
undefined
because CDG-coalgebras are
not complexes and do not have cohomology spaces.

Similarly to Section
4.4
,
the problem is resolved by considering
filtered
quasi-isomorphisms
, which actually make sense for CDG-coalgebras.
An
admissible filtration
on a coaugmented CDG-coalgebra

(C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}},\gamma)
is defined as an exhaustive comultiplicative
increasing filtration by graded vector subspaces
F_{n}C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}\subset C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}

such that
F_{-1}C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}=0
,
F_{0}C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}=\gamma(k)
, and

d(F_{n}C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}})\subset F_{n}C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
for all
n\geq 0
.

In particular, any coaugmented CDG-coalgebra having an admissible
filtration is conilpotent.
Conversely, the canonical increasing filtration (as in
Section
3.3
) on any conilpotent
CDG-coalgebra is admissible.

For any admissible filtration
F
on a coaugmented CDG-coalgebra

(C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}},\gamma)
one has
d^{2}(F_{n}C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}})\subset F_{n-1}C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
for
all
n\geq 0
, since
d^{2}(c)=h*c-c*h
for all
c\in C
and
the curvature linear function
h\colon C\longrightarrow k
annihilates
F_{0}C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
.
Consequently, the induced differential
d
on the associated graded
vector space (in fact, the associated bigraded coalgebra)

\mathrm{gr}^{F}C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}=\bigoplus_{n=0}^{\infty}F_{n}C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}/F_{n-1}C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
squares to
zero, so it makes
\mathrm{gr}^{F}C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
a complex, and in fact,
a (bigraded, uncurved) DG-coalgebra.

Let
(f,a)\colon C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}\longrightarrow D^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
be a morphism of conilpotent
CDG-coalgebras.
The morphism
(f,a)
is said to be a
filtered quasi-isomorphism

if there exist admissible filtrations
F
on both the CDG-coalgebras

C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
and
D^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
such that
f(F_{n}C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}})\subset F_{n}D^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
and
the induced map
F_{n}C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}/F_{n-1}C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}\longrightarrow F_{n}D^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}/F_{n-1}D^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}

is a quasi-isomorphism of complexes of vector spaces for every
n\geq 0
.
Equivalently, this means that
\mathrm{gr}^{F}f\colon\mathrm{gr}^{F}C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}\longrightarrow\mathrm{gr}^{F}D^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}

is a quasi-isomorphism of (bigraded) DG-coalgebras.

Theorem 6.6
.

Let
\mathsf{Quis}
be the class of all quasi-isomorphisms of (nonzero)
DG-algebras and
\mathsf{FQuis}
be the class of all filtered
quasi-isomorphisms of conilpotent CDG-coalgebras.
Then the adjoint functors
\Br^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}_{v}
and
\Cb^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\gamma}
induce
mutually inverse equivalences of categories

\Br^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}_{v}\colon k{\operatorname{\mathsf{--alg}}}_{\mathsf{dg}}^{+}[\mathsf{Quis}^{-1}]\,\simeq\,k{\operatorname{\mathsf{--coalg}}}_{\mathsf{cdg}}^{\mathsf{conilp}}[\mathsf{FQuis}^{-1}]\,:\!\Cb^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\gamma}.

Proof.

This is
[
64
, Theorem 6.10(a)]
.
∎

Example 6.7
.

The following trivial example may be instructive.
Notice that there are many nonzero DG-algebras
quasi-isomorphic

to the zero DG-algebra; they are all legitimate objects of
the category
k{\operatorname{\mathsf{--alg}}}_{\mathsf{dg}}^{+}
.
One can easily see that all such DG-algebras are isomorphic in
the category
k{\operatorname{\mathsf{--alg}}}_{\mathsf{dg}}^{+}[\mathsf{Quis}^{-1}]
.
Typical representatives of this isomorphism class are the DG-algebra

A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}=(k[\varepsilon],d)
, with
\varepsilon\in A^{-1}
and

d(\varepsilon)=1
, and its quotient DG-algebra
\overline{A}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}=(k[\varepsilon]/(\varepsilon^{2}),d)
.
For any (unital) DG-algebra
X^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
quasi-isomorphic to zero, there
exists a morphism of (unital) DG-algebras
A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\longrightarrow X^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
; so

X^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
is isomorphic to
A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
in
k{\operatorname{\mathsf{--alg}}}_{\mathsf{dg}}^{+}[\mathsf{Quis}^{-1}]
.

One may wonder what the corresponding conilpotent CDG-coalgebras are.
Let
B^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}=(k[h],0,h)
be the CDG-algebra over
k
whose underlying
graded algebra is the algebra of polynomials in the curvature
element
h
(while the differential is
d=0
).
As
B^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
is a graded algebra with finite-dimensional components,
the graded dual vector space to
B^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
has a natural structure of
CDG-coalgebra, which we denote by
C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
.
So one has
C^{n}\simeq k
whenever
n
is an even nonpositive
integer, and
C^{n}=0
otherwise; the curvature linear function

h\colon C^{-2}\longrightarrow k
is an isomorphism, the differential on
C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}

vanishes, and the comultiplication map
C^{-2n}\longrightarrow C^{-2p}\otimes_{k}C^{-2q}
is an isomorphism for all
p+q=n
,
p
,
q\geq 0
.
One can easily see that
C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}=\Br^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}_{v}(\overline{A}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})
, where

v\colon\overline{A}\longrightarrow k
is the unique homogeneous retraction onto
the unit line.
(In particular,
C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
is obviously a conilpotent CDG-coalgebra.)

Furthermore, consider the CDG-algebra
\overline{B}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}=(k[h]/(h^{2}),0,h)
.
The graded dual vector space
\overline{C}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
to
\overline{B}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}

is a CDG-coalgebra with
\overline{C}^{n}=k
for
n=0
and
n=-2
,
and
\overline{C}^{n}=0
for all other
n
.
The curvature linear function
h\colon\overline{C}^{-2}\longrightarrow k
is
an isomorphism, while the differential on
\overline{C}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
vanishes.
It is easy to see that
A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}=\Cb^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\gamma}(\overline{C}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}})
, where

\gamma\colon k\longrightarrow\overline{C}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
is the unique coaugmentation of
the conilpotent CDG-coalgebra
\overline{C}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
.

The CDG-algebras
B^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
and
\overline{B}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
were discussed
in
[
44
, Section 2.2 and Proposition 3.2]
under the name of
“initial CDG-algebras”.
In fact,
B^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
is the initial object of the category of
CDG-algebras over
k
and
strict
morphisms between them,
i. e., morphisms
(f,0)
with a vanishing change-of-connection
element
a=0
.
By the same token, the CDG-coalgebras
C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
and
\overline{C}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}

might be called “terminal CDG-coalgebras” (and indeed, the zero
DG-algebra is the terminal object of the category
k{\operatorname{\mathsf{--alg}}}_{\mathsf{dg}}
).

Similarly to the discussion in
Section
4.5
,
the equivalence of categories in
Theorem
6.6
can be
expressed as a Quillen equivalence of model category structures.
One additional complication as compared to
Section
4.5
,
which arises here, is that the category
k{\operatorname{\mathsf{--alg}}}_{\mathsf{dg}}^{+}
of nonzero
DG-algebras has no terminal object (because the zero DG-algebra, which
is the terminal object of
k{\operatorname{\mathsf{--alg}}}_{\mathsf{dg}}
, is excluded from
k{\operatorname{\mathsf{--alg}}}_{\mathsf{dg}}^{+}
).
The category
k{\operatorname{\mathsf{--coalg}}}_{\mathsf{cdg}}^{\mathsf{conilp}}
of conilpotent CDG-coalgebras
has no terminal object, either (as only the category of conilpotent
CDG-coalgebras and
strict
morphisms between them has a terminal
object, mentioned in Example
6.7

above).

Still the definition of a model category (as in
[
33
]
) requires
existence of all limits and colimits.
This problem is resolved by “finalizing” (formally adjoining terminal
objects to) both the categories
k{\operatorname{\mathsf{--alg}}}_{\mathsf{dg}}^{+}
and

k{\operatorname{\mathsf{--coalg}}}_{\mathsf{cdg}}^{\mathsf{conilp}}
before defining model structures on them.
We refer to
[
64
, Section 9.2, Theorem 9.3(a), and end of
Section 9.3]
for the details.

6.8.
Twisting cochains in the curved context

Let
V^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}=(V,d_{V})
and
W^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}=(W,d_{W})
be two precomplexes, i. e.,
graded
k
-vector spaces endowed with homogeneous
k
-linear
differentials
d_{V}\colon V\longrightarrow V
and
d_{W}\colon W\longrightarrow W
of degree
1

with possibly nonzero squares (as in
Section
6.3
).
Then the precomplex
\Hom^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}_{k}(V^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}},W^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}})
is defined as
the graded Hom space
\Hom_{k}(V,W)
endowed with the differential

d\colon\Hom_{k}(V,W)\longrightarrow\Hom_{k}(V,W)
of degree
1
given by the usual
formula
d(f)(x)=d_{W}(f(x))-(-1)^{|f|}f(d_{V}(x))
for all

f\in\Hom_{k}^{|f|}(V,W)
and
x\in V^{|x|}
.

Let
C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}=(C,d_{C},h_{C})
be a CDG-coalgebra and
B^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}=(B,d_{B},h_{B})

be a CDG-algebra over
k
.
Consider the graded Hom space
\Hom_{k}(C,B)
, and endow it with
a graded
k
-algebra structure as constructed in
Section
5.1

and a differential
d
given by the usual rule as above.
Furthermore, let
h\in\Hom^{2}_{k}(C,B)
be the element given by
the formula
h(c)=\epsilon(c)h_{B}-h_{C}(c)\cdot 1
for all
c\in C
,
where
1\in B
is the unit element and
\epsilon\colon C\longrightarrow k
is
the counit map.
Then
\Hom^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}_{k}(C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}},B^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}})=(\Hom_{k}(C,B),d,h)
is a CDG-algebra
over
k

[
64
, Section 6.2]
.

Let
E^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}=(E,d,h)
be a CDG-ring over
k
.
Then an element
a\in E^{1}
is said to be a
Maurer–Cartan
element
if it satisfies the equation
a^{2}+d(a)+h=0
in
E^{2}
.
Equivalently,
a\in E^{1}
is a Maurer–Cartan element if and only
if there exists an odd derivation
d^{\prime}\colon E\longrightarrow E
of degree
1

(specifically,
d^{\prime}(e)=d(e)+[a,e]
for all
e\in E
) such that

(\mathrm{id}_{E},a)\colon(E,d^{\prime},0)\longrightarrow(E,d,h)
is an isomorphism of CDG-rings.
So Maurer–Cartan elements in a CDG-ring parametrize its
change-of-connection isomorphisms with DG-rings.

By the definition, a
twisting cochain

\tau
for
a CDG-coalgebra
C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
and a CDG-algebra
B^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
over
k
is
a Maurer–Cartan element in the CDG-algebra
\Hom_{k}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}(C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}},B^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}})
.
So
\tau\colon C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}\longrightarrow B^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
is a homogeneous
k
-linear map of
degree
1
satisfying the Maurer–Cartan equation.
(There is a slight abuse of terminology involved here, as
by “cochains” one usually means elements in grading components
of a complex, while
\Hom_{k}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}(C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}},B^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}})
is not a complex.)

This definition of a twisting cochain is a generalization of the one
in Section
5.1
.
Similarly to the discussion in
Section
5.1
,
various conditions of compatibility with (co)augmentations may be
imposed on twisting cochains.
We will be particularly interested in the context where

(C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}},\gamma)
is a coaugmented (better yet, conilpotent)
CDG-coalgebra, while
A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
is a DG-algebra.
In this case, twisting cochains
\tau\colon C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}\longrightarrow A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
such that

\tau\circ\gamma=0
play an important role.

Examples 6.8
.

(1) This is a nonaugmented version of
Example
5.1
(1).
Let
A\neq 0
be an algebra over
k
and
C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}=\Br^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}_{v}(A)
be its
bar-construction, as in
Section
6.4
.
Let
\gamma\colon k\longrightarrow C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
be the natural coaugmentation of

\Br^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}_{v}(A)
(as in
Section
6.7
).
Then the composition
\tau
of the direct summand projection

\Br(A)\longrightarrow A_{+}^{\otimes 1}=A_{+}
and the inclusion
A_{+}\simeq\ker(v)\longrightarrow A
is a twisting cochain for the CDG-coalgebra
C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}

and the algebra
A
(viewed as a (C)DG-algebra in the obvious way).
The equation of compatibility with the coaugmentation

\tau\circ\gamma=0
is satisfied in this example.

(2) This is a nonaugmented version of
Example
5.1
(2).
Let
A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
be a nonzero DG-algebra and
C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}=\Br^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}_{v}(A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})

be its bar-construction, as in
Section
6.5
.
Let
\gamma\colon k\longrightarrow C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
denote the natural coaugmentation of

\Br^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}_{v}(A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})
.
Then the composition
\tau\colon C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}\longrightarrow A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
of the direct summand
projection
\Br(A)\longrightarrow A_{+}^{\otimes 1}=A_{+}
and
the inclusion
A_{+}\simeq\ker(v)\longrightarrow A
is a twisting cochain for
the CDG-coalgebra
C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
and the DG-algebra
A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
.
The equation
\tau\circ\gamma=0
is satisfied for this twisting
cochain.

Example 6.9
.

This is a curved version of
Example
5.2
(2).
Let
(C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}},\gamma)
be a coaugmented CDG-coalgebra, and let

A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}=\Cb^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\gamma}(C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}})
be its cobar-construction, as in
Section
6.6
.
Then the composition
\tau\colon C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}\longrightarrow A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
of the natural
surjection
C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}\longrightarrow C^{{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}},+}=C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}/\gamma(k)
and
the direct summand inclusion
C^{{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}},+}\longrightarrow\Cb_{\gamma}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}})

is a twisting cochain for the CDG-coalgebra
C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
and
the DG-algebra
A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
.
The equation
\tau\circ\gamma=0
is satisfied for this twisting
cochain.

Example 6.10
.

This is a noncoaugmented version of the previous example.
Let
C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}\neq 0
be a CDG-coalgebra, and let
B^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}=\Cb^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}_{w}(C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}})

be its (noncoaugmented) cobar-construction, as in
Section
6.6
.
Then the composition
\tau\colon C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}\longrightarrow B^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
of the surjection

C\longrightarrow\coker(w)\simeq C_{+}
and the direct summand inclusion

C_{+}=C_{+}^{\otimes 1}\longrightarrow\Cb(C)
is a twisting cochain for
the CDG-coalgebra
C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
and the CDG-algebra
B^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
.

Hint of proof of
Lemma
6.5
.

Let
A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
be a nonzero DG-algebra and
C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
be a conilpotent
CDG-coalgebra over
k
.
Then both the DG-algebra homomorphisms
\Cb^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\gamma}(C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}})\allowbreak\longrightarrow A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
and the (coaugmented) CDG-coalgebra
morphisms
C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}\longrightarrow\Br^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}_{v}(A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})
correspond bijectively to
twisting cochains
\tau\colon C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}\longrightarrow A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
satisfying the equation
of compatibility with the coaugmentation
\tau\circ\gamma=0
.
∎

Let
A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
be a nonzero DG-algebra and
(C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}},\gamma)
be
a conilpotent CDG-coalgebra.
Let
\tau\colon C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}\longrightarrow A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
be a twisting cochain satisfying
the equation
\tau\circ\gamma=0
.
Generalizing the definition from
Section
5.2
,
the twisting cochain
\tau
is said to be
acyclic
if one of
(or equivalently, both) the related DG-algebra homomorphism

\Cb^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\gamma}(C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}})\longrightarrow A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
and the CDG-coalgebra
morphism
C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}\longrightarrow\Br^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}_{v}(A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})
become isomorphisms after
the quasi-isomorphisms of DG-algebras, or respectively
the filtered quasi-isomorphisms of conilpotent CDG-coalgebras,
are inverted, as in
Theorem
6.6
.
Simply put, the twisting cochain
\tau
is called acyclic if
the related homomorphism of DG-algebras
\Cb^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\gamma}(C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}})\longrightarrow A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
is a quasi-isomorphism.

For example, the twisting cochain from
Example
6.9

is acyclic, by the definition, whenever the coaugmented
CDG-coalgebra
(C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}},\gamma)
is conilpotent.
It is a part of
Theorem
6.6

that the twisting cochain from
Examples
6.8
is
acyclic for any nonzero algebra
A
or DG-algebra
A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
.

Example 6.11
.

The following example is a nonaugmented version of
Section
2.5

and Example
5.3
.

Let
(A,F)
be a nonhomogeneous quadratic algebra, as defined
in Section
2.5
,
and let
C=(\mathrm{gr}^{F}A)^{?}
be the quadratic dual coalgebra to
the quadratic algebra
\mathrm{gr}^{F}A
.
Let
v\colon A\longrightarrow k
be a
k
-linear retraction onto the unit line.
Then there is a natural isomorphism of vector spaces
\mathrm{gr}^{F}_{1}A=F_{1}A/F_{0}A\simeq\ker(v)\cap F_{1}A\subset A
.
So the vector space
V=\mathrm{gr}^{F}_{1}A
can be viewed as a subspace in

A_{+}=A/k\cdot 1\simeq\ker(v)
, and the graded coalgebra
C
can be
viewed as a subcoalgebra in
\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$T$}(A_{+})
,

C\subset\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$T$}(V)\subset\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$T$}(A_{+})=\Br(A).

Similarly to
Section
2.5
,
one observes that the subcoalgebra
C\subset\Br(A)
is in fact
a CDG-subcoalgebra in the CDG-coalgebra
\Br^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}_{v}(A)
constructed
in Section
6.4
,

C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}\subset\Br^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}_{v}(A)
.
Quite simply, this means that the differential
\partial
on

\Br^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}_{v}(A)
preserves
C
, that is
\partial(C)\subset C
.
The curvature linear function
h\colon C\longrightarrow k
is produced as
the restriction of the curvature linear function
h_{\Br}\colon\Br(A)\longrightarrow k
to the subcoalgebra
C\subset\Br(A)
.
This is another way to spell out the nonhomogeneous quadratic
duality construction of
[
61
, Proposition 2.2]

or
[
58
, Proposition 4.1 in Chapter 5]
.

We have constructed the CDG-coalgebra
C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}=(C,\partial,h)
nonhomogeneous
quadratic dual to a nonhomogeneous quadratic algebra
(A,F)
.
Now the composition
C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}\longrightarrow\Br^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}_{v}(A)\longrightarrow A
of
the inclusion map
C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}\longrightarrow\Br^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}_{v}(A)
with the twisting
cochain
\Br^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}_{v}(A)\longrightarrow A
from
Example
6.8
(1) is
a twisting cochain
\tau\colon C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}\longrightarrow A
.
Equivalently, the twisting cochain
\tau
can be constructed as
the composition of the direct summand projection
C\longrightarrow C^{1}=F_{1}A/F_{0}A
and the inclusion
F_{1}A/F_{0}A\simeq\ker(v)\cap F_{1}A\longrightarrow A
.

The direct summand inclusion
\gamma\colon k=C_{0}\longrightarrow C
is
a coaugmentation of the CDG-coalgebra
C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
, so
(C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}},\gamma)

is a coaugmented, and in fact, conilpotent CDG-coalgebra.
The twisting cochain
\tau\colon C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}\longrightarrow A
satisfies the equation

\tau\circ\gamma=0
.

For any nonhomogeneous
Koszul
algebra
(A,F)
, as defined
in Section
2.5

(i. e., a nonhomogeneous quadratic algebra for which the quadratic
graded algebra
\mathrm{gr}^{F}A
is Koszul), the twisting cochain

\tau\colon C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}\longrightarrow A
is acyclic.
As in Section
5.2
,
this claim is a corollary of the proof of Poincaré–Birkhoff–Witt
theorem for nonhomogeneous Koszul algebras
in
[
61
, Section 3.2–3.3]
or
[
58
, Proposition 7.2(ii)
in Chapter 5]
.

A generalization of these constructions and the acyclicity claim
above to filtered
DG-algebras

(A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}},F)
can be found
in
[
64
, Section 6.6]
.

6.9.
Derived Koszul duality on the comodule side

Let
B^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
be a CDG-algebra and
C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
be a CDG-coalgebra
over
k
, and let
\tau\colon C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}\longrightarrow B^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
be a twisting cochain.

Given a left CDG-comodule
N^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
over
C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
, we consider
the tensor product of graded vector spaces
B\otimes_{k}N
and endow
it with a differential
d
twisted with the twisting cochain
\tau

using the same formulas as in
Sections
5.3
–
5.4
.
Then
B^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}\otimes^{\tau}N^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}=(B\otimes_{k}N,\>d)
is a left CDG-module
over
B^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
.

Similarly, given a left CDG-module
M^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
over
B^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
, we consider
the tensor product of graded vector spaces
C\otimes_{k}M
and endow
it with a differential
d
twisted with the twisting cochain
\tau

using the same formulas as in
Sections
5.3
–
5.4
.
Then
C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}\otimes^{\tau}M^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}=(C\otimes_{k}M,\>d)
is a left CDG-comodule
over
C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
.

For example, let
A
be a nonzero associative algebra and

C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}=\Br^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}_{v}(A)
be its bar-construction, as in
Section
6.4
.
Let
M
be a left
A
-module.
Then the CDG-module
\Br^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}_{v}(A,M)
over
\Br^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}_{v}(A)
constructed
in Section
6.4

can be recovered as
\Br^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}_{v}(A,M)=\Br^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}_{v}(A)\otimes^{\tau}M
, where

\tau\colon C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}\longrightarrow A
is the twisting cochain from
Example
6.8
(1).

Recall the notation
B^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}
for the DG-category of left
CDG-modules over
B^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
and
C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--comod}}}
for the DG-category
of left CDG-comodules over
C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
(see
Sections
6.2
–
6.3
).
Similarly to Section
5.4
,
one observes that the DG-functor

B^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}\otimes^{\tau}{-}\,:C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--comod}}}\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5muB^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}

is left adjoint to the DG-functor

C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}\otimes^{\tau}{-}\,:B^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5muC^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--comod}}}.

Theorem 6.12
.

Let
A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
be a nonzero DG-algebra and
(C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}},\gamma)
be
a conilpotent CDG-coalgebra over
k
(as defined in
Section
6.7
).
Let
\tau\colon C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}\longrightarrow A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
be an acyclic twisting cochain
(as defined in Section
6.8
);
this includes the condition that
\tau\circ\gamma=0
.
Then the adjoint functors
M^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\longmapsto C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}\otimes^{\tau}M^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}

and
N^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}\longmapsto A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\otimes^{\tau}N^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
induce a triangulated
equivalence between the conventional
derived
category of
left DG-modules over
A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
and the
coderived
category of
left CDG-comodules over
C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
,

\mathsf{D}(A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}})\simeq\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{co}}(C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--comod}}}).

Proof.

This is
[
64
, Theorem 6.5(a)]
.
The particular case corresponding to the twisting cochain from
Examples
6.8
can be
found in
[
64
, Theorem 6.3(a)]
, while
the case of the twisting cochain from
Example
6.9

(for a conilpotent CDG-coalgebra
C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
)
is considered in
[
64
, Theorem 6.4(a)]
.
For the definition of the coderived category,
see Section
7.7
below.
∎

The triangulated equivalence of
Theorem
6.12
takes
the free left DG-module
A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
over
A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
to the left
CDG-comodule
k
over
C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
(with the
C
-comodule structure
on
k
defined in terms of the coaugmentation
\gamma
).
Notice that,
unlike
in
Theorems
5.4
–
5.5
, there is no natural
structure of a DG-module over
A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
on the one-dimensional
vector space
k
, as the DG-algebra
A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
is not augmented.
This fact is not unrelated to the fact that there is no natural
structure of a left CDG-comodule over
C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
on the cofree graded
left
C
-comodule
C
(for the reason explained at the end of
Section
6.3
).

Theorem 6.13
.

Let
C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
be a nonzero CDG-coalgebra, and let
\tau\colon C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}\longrightarrow\Cb^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}_{w}(C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}})=B^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
be the twisting cochain from
Example
6.10
.
Then the adjoint functors
M^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}\longmapsto C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}\otimes^{\tau}M^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}

and
N^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}\longmapsto B^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}\otimes^{\tau}N^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
induce a triangulated
equivalence between the
absolute derived
category of left
CDG-modules over
B^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
and the
coderived
category of
left CDG-comodules over
C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
,

\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{abs}}(B^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}})\simeq\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{co}}(C^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--comod}}}).

Proof.

This is
[
64
, Theorem 6.7(a)]
.
The definitions of the coderived and absolute derived categories will
be explained in Sections
7.6
–
7.7
below.
The absolute derived category
\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{abs}}(B^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}})
coincides with
the coderived category
\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{co}}(B^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}})

by
[
64
, Theorem 3.6(a)]
; see
Theorem
7.8
(a) below.
∎

For extended versions of
Theorems
6.12

and
6.13

(“Koszul triality”), see Section
9.7
.
Some additional comments on the proofs of the theorems will be
offered in Section
9.8
.

A version of nonconilpotent Koszul duality
(Theorem
6.13
) with
the roles of the algebras and coalgebras switched (starting from
a DG-algebra
A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
and considering the cofree nonconilpotent
coalgebra spanned by
A_{+}
, or rather, the vector space dual
topological algebra) was suggested in the paper
[
28
]
.
For a version of derived nonhomogeneous Koszul duality for

DG-categories
, see
[
32
]
.

7.
Derived Categories of the Second Kind

“Derived categories of the second kind” is a common name for
the
coderived
,
contraderived
, and
absolute derived

categories, while the conventional
derived
category is called
the “derived category of the first kind”.
The
classical homological algebra
can be described as
the domain where there is
no difference
between derived
categories of the first and the second kind.
Derived categories of the second kind play a crucial role in all
formulations of derived Koszul duality outside of the contexts
in which assumptions of boundedness of the (internal or cohomological)
grading ensure applicability of the classical homological algebra.

7.1.
Importance of derived categories of the second kind

The earliest versions of derived Koszul duality, establishing
the very existence of the phenomenon, were formulated for complexes
(DG-algebras, etc.) up to conventional quasi-isomorphism.
This includes Quillen’s equivalence between the categories of
negatively cohomologically graded Lie DG-algebras and
connected, simply connected, negatively cohomologically graded
cocommutative DG-coalgebras over a field of characteristic
zero
[
84
, Theorem I]
.
In this result, the boundedness conditions on the cohomological
grading allow to avoid the problem demonstrated in
Example
1.1
above
(cf. our Theorems
4.2

and
6.6
, where
the cohomological gradings are completely unbounded, but
the notion of a
filtered quasi-isomorphism
is used).
See, e. g.,
[
67
, Section 3]
for noncommutative versions of
Quillen’s theory.

The most important area of derived Koszul duality results for
which the conventional quasi-isomorphisms are sufficient is
the
homogeneous
Koszul duality.
This means the study of complexes of modules, DG-modules etc. endowed with an additional
internal
grading which is
assumed to be positive or negative on the rings and bounded below
or bounded above on the modules.
In some classical contexts, the modules are assumed to be finitely
generated, which serves to ensure that their internal gradings
are bounded.
This theory goes back to the paper
[
8
]
(see
also
[
55
, Appendix A]
), where the
derived

S
-
\Lambda
duality
(the equivalence between the bounded
derived categories of finitely generated graded modules over
the symmetric algebra of a finite-dimensional vector space and
the exterior algebra of the dual vector space) was introduced.
A standard reasonably modern source on derived homogeneous
Koszul duality is
[
7
, Section 2.12]
; another exposition
can be found in
[
64
, Appendix A]
.

To give a simple explanation of the role of positive or bounded
internal grading, notice that
\Tor^{A}_{0}(k,M)\neq 0
for any bounded
below graded module
M\neq 0
over a positively graded algebra

A=k\oplus A_{1}\oplus A_{2}\oplus\dotsb
.
This serves to exclude counterexamples such as in
Examples
1.2
.
Say, one can take
A=k[x]
to be the polynomial (symmetric) algebra in
one variable
x
, internally graded so that the generator
x
sits in
the internal grading
1
, and endowed with the augmentation

\alpha\colon A\longrightarrow k
induced by the internal grading (so
\alpha(x)=0
).
Then all the counterexamples of modules
M
in
Example
1.2
(2) are either ungraded, or
their grading is unbounded.

When the conventional quasi-isomorphism is
not
suitable as
the equivalence relation on one of the sides (which is usually, but
not always, the coalgebra or comodule/contramodule side) of Koszul
duality, another equivalence relation on the respective side has to be
defined instead.
When the equivalence relation on the other (usually, the algebra or
module side) of the duality is still the conventional quasi-isomorphism,
a possibility of quick hack presents itself: call a morphism on
the coalgebra side a “weak equivalence” if the Koszul duality functor
transforms it into a quasi-isomorphism on the algebra side.
In this approach, the Koszul duality becomes an equivalence of
two categories one of which is constructed partially in terms of
the other one.
Such formulations of Koszul duality can be found in
the paper
[
30
, Theorem 3.2]
,
the dissertation
[
47
, Théorème 2.2.2.2]
, and
the note
[
42
, Section 4]
, as well as in
the preprint
[
6
, Section 7.2]
.

The latter reference concerns the
D
-
\Omega
duality
,
that is, Koszul duality between the rings of differential operators
and the de Rham (C)DG-rings of differential forms (which is
a thematic example of
relative
nonhomogeneous Koszul
duality
[
75
]
.)
For another early approach to the
D
-
\Omega
duality,
see
[
39
]
.
Such approaches were tried in the absence of understanding of
the existence of and the role of the constructions of
the
derived categories of the second kind
.

7.2.
History of derived categories of the second kind, I

Let me start with some personal reminiscences.
A shorter historical account of the same events can be found
in
[
81
, Remark 9.2]
.

The problem of constructing derived nonhomogeneous Koszul duality
(for nonhomogeneous Koszul algebras, as in
Section
2.5
)
was suggested to me by Misha Finkelberg sometime around 1991–92,
in a handwritten letter sent to Moscow (where I lived) from
Massachusetts (where he was doing his Ph. D. studies at Harvard).
Alongside with A. Vaintrob, Misha was my main informal de facto
teacher and advisor in the second half of ’80s and early ’90s.

Misha wrote that the problem of derived nonhomogeneous Koszul duality
(i. e., constructing a derived equivalence between complexes of
modules over a nonhomogeneous Koszul algebra and DG-modules over
the nonhomogeneous quadratic dual DG-algebra) would be a natural
extension of my work on nonhomogeneous quadratic
duality
[
61
,
58
]
.
(The paper
[
61
]
was only submitted in November 1992, but very
early short versions of what became the book
[
58
]
circulated
since 1991, if not 1990.)
The case of a finite-dimensional Lie algebra and its
Chevalley–Eilenberg DG-algebra (as in
Example
2.5
) was perceived as a thematic
example, of course; and the idea to proceed further to

D
-
\Omega
duality was suggested in Misha’s letter.

Soon, that is also around 1992, I made an intensive attempt to attack
and conquer the problem, which resulted in a tentative understanding
that the derived category
\mathsf{D}(\mathfrak{g}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}})
was equivalent to the homotopy
category of DG-modules over
\Lambda^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(\mathfrak{g}^{*})
with injective (or
equivalently, projective) underlying graded
\Lambda(\mathfrak{g}^{*})
-modules.
From the contemporary point of view (say, after Becker’s
paper
[
5
]
), that was about the best answer one could hope for.
Back in the ’90s, I did not see it that way.

Following my thinking of the early ’90s, derived nonhomogeneous Koszul
duality was supposed to be an equivalence between
Verdier
quotient categories
of the homotopy categories of DG-modules, not
their
subcategories
.
I wanted a
derived
Koszul duality, not a
homotopy
one!
(in the sense of the homotopy categories of complexes or DG-modules,
that is, closed morphisms up to cochain homotopy).
Specifically, whatever the construction of a category of DG-modules
over
\Lambda^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(\mathfrak{g}^{*})
equivalent to
\mathsf{D}(\mathfrak{g}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}
) would be,
there was supposed to be a way to assign an object of this category
to an arbitrary DG-module over
\Lambda^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(\mathfrak{g}^{*})
.
I did not see a natural way to assign a graded-injective DG-module
to an arbitrary one.
Once again, Becker’s
[
5
, Proposition 1.3.6]
did not exist
back then.

By late 90’s, I perceived derived nonhomogeneous Koszul duality as
an important unsolved, perhaps unsolvable, problem obstructing
development of the theory of DG-modules generally.
In the meantime, I moved from Moscow to the U.S., first as a visitor,
then as a graduate student, and finally as a postdoc.
My access to the literature improved, and I was able to observe that
the theory was developing nevertheless.
On top of Spaltenstein’s paper
[
86
]
, which was known to me
back in early ’90s, there appeared an important paper of
Keller
[
41
]
.
I had also noticed several papers by Neeman (e. g.,
[
10
,
51
]
),
from which I learned the concepts of homotopy (co)limits and
the telescope construction in triangulated categories, as well
as compactly generated triangulated categories and other things.

In the 1998–99 academic year I was an NSF postdoc at the Institute
for Advanced Study.
My postdoc advisors there were P. Deligne and V. Voevodsky.
In March 99 my term as a postdoc was coming to an end, and Voevodsky
suggested that we should meet for a discussion.
At the meeting, Voevodsky returned to the topic of DG-algebra and
DG-module theory (which he occasionally mentioned to me during our
conversations over the years, starting from 1994).
His idea was to develop a DG version of Tannaka theory for
conservative functors from an (enhanced) triangulated category to
the (derived) category of vector spaces, with applications to his
derived categories of motives and motivic realization functors
in mind.

Discussing the problems involved, we arrived to the topic of
a Koszul duality between ungraded modules over the symmetric and
exterior algebras; and I mentioned that it was an unsolved problem,
as the naïve approach with conventional derived categories
on both sides of the would-be equivalence did not work.
Upon hearing my arguments, Voevodsky immediately replied that this
problem can be solved, and must be solved.
I went home and solved it.
It was immediately obvious to me that an important discovery was made.
By mid-April 99, I had both the definition of what is now called
the coderived category
(Definitions
7.5

and
7.11
below), and the proofs
of derived nonhomogeneous Koszul duality results such as
Theorems
2.1
,

2.4
,

2.6
,
and
2.7
.

Having made my discovery, I went to the IAS library to look for
relevant prior literature.
The most important thing I found was the remarkable series of
papers by Eilenberg and Moore
[
21
,
22
,
23
]
, with the final
piece of the series authored by Husemoller, Moore, and
Stasheff
[
34
]
.
In particular, the 1962 paper
[
21
]
clarified the issue of
“divergent spectral sequences”, which was how the difficulties
in Koszul duality were spoken of in my Moscow circles in
the early ’90s (see Remarks
7.1

and
7.2
below for
a discussion).

The 1974 paper
[
34
]
introduced the distinction between what
it called the
differential derived functors of the first
and

the second kind
.
I immediately realized that it was relevant to my exotic derived
categories of DG-modules and DG-comodules; so I seized on this
terminology and started to call my categories “derived categories
of the second kind” (as opposed to the conventional derived category,
which was the “derived category of the first kind”).

Only after my definitions of derived categories of the second kind
were already invented, I discovered Hinich’s papers
[
29
,
30
]
.
My recollection is that I may have also reinvented Hinich’s
definition of a filtered quasi-isomorphism of conilpotent
DG-coalgebras before reading it in
[
30
]
.

That
direct sums of injective modules
and
direct products
of projective modules
play an important role in my constructions of
derived categories of the second kind for DG-modules, I also
well realized in Spring 1999.
In this connection, I found references such as the papers
[
16
]

and
[
4
]
(explaining when the products of projective modules are
projective), which were eventually cited in the memoir
[
64
]
.
But the more advanced conditions (
*
) and (
{*}{*}
)
from
[
64
, Sections 3.7 and 3.8]

(see Theorem
7.9
below)
were only invented in 2009.

In fact, the condition that the direct sums of injective modules are
injective characterized Noetherian rings, which in my thinking of
the time was general enough for an interesting theory (cf.
[
46
]
).
But the rings over which products of projective modules are projective
turned out to be more rare (as per
[
4
,
16
]
), essentially only
Artinian rings and their immediate generalizations.
This created a misconception that the contraderived categories were
rarely well-behaved outside of the realms of contramodules over
coalgebras over fields and modules over Artinian rings, which haunted
me for the years to come (until Spring 2012,
when
[
65
, Appendix B]
was largely written and the notion of

contraherent cosheaves
was discovered).

1998–99 was a year of Geometric Representation Theory at the IAS.
My postdoc was not a part of that Special Program, but many
mathematicians I knew, including Finkelberg and other people from
Moscow, were Members of the IAS for that academic year as a part of
that program.
In the Spring 99 we had an informal seminar where I presented my new
results on Koszul duality in the form of a series of talks.
Subsequently I spoke about these results at various seminars
(including H.-J. Baues’ Oberseminar Topologie at MPIM-Bonn
in July 2001).

The first preprint versions of what became my memoir
[
64
]

only appeared in Spring 2009.
There were several reasons for such a protracted delay, the most
important of them being that the task of typing an original research
exposition of the size and complexity of
[
64
]
was way
above my abilities in the first half of ’00s.
My early research work consisted of very short papers; writing
longer texts required a different technique.
An acute problem of insufficient mathematical writing skills, which
tormented me since Fall 1995, was only resolved by about Winter 2006/07.
But there was also a terminological problem.

Naming things is important.
The expression “derived category of the second kind” was both too
long and too imprecise, as there were at least two important
“derived categories of the second kind” known to me in Spring 1999
already.
Now they are called the
coderived
and
contraderived

categories.
I was unable to invent such a nomenclature myself.
What I had in Spring 99 was not a terminology for the distinction
between the two dual concepts of a coderived and a contraderived
category, but only a
notation
, and not a very good one at that.
The coderived category was denoted by
\mathsf{D}^{\prime}
, and the contraderived
category by
\mathsf{D}^{\prime\prime}
(one can still see traces of this notation in
the old letters
[
62
]
and the recent paper
[
70
]
).
A work such as
[
64
]
could not be written in a readable form
with such clumsy terminological tools.

The terminology “coderived category” first appeared in
Keller’s note
[
42
]
.
One of the mathematicians in Moscow directed my attention to this
note sometime in mid-’00s.
Upon seeing that the terminological problem had been solved, I knew
that the time had come for me to write up my results.
I started with a treatise on semi-infinite homological
algebra
[
63
]
, which was much heavier with lots of details that
I could not keep all in my mind, but could only work out in writing;
so I had more motivation to type that work.
A very elaborate terminological system featuring the prefixes “co-”
and “contra-” (and also “semi-”) was developed for the purposes of
the book
[
63
]
based on the idea I borrowed from Keller’s note.

I typed the bulk of the material in
[
64
]
while being
a visitor at the IHES in March–April 2009.
I contacted Bernhard Keller by e-mail from Bures and went to visit
him in his office in Paris, and subsequently gave a talk at
the Algebra Seminar in the Institut Henri Poincaré.
The title of the talk was “Koszul Triality”.
This talk is mentioned in the acknowledgement to
[
44
]
.

7.3.
Philosophy of derived categories of the second kind, I

A complex (e. g., a complex of modules or a DG-module)
K^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}

can be thought of in two ways.
One can view
K^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
as a deformation of its
cohomology module

H^{*}(K^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})
, or as a deformation of its
underlying graded
module

K
(say, endowed with the zero differential).
In the notation of
[
64
]
(going back
to
[
34
, Remark I.5.1]
), the important operation of forgetting
the differential is denoted by the upper index
\#
; so one can
write
K=K^{{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}{}^{\#}
.

These two ways of looking at a complex are reflected in the classical
concept of
two spectral sequences of
hypercohomology

[
15
, Section XVII.3]
.
The old word “hypercohomology” means the cohomology groups one
obtains by applying a derived functor to a complex (rather than just
to a module).
Let
F
be a left exact functor and
K^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
be a complex of modules,
bounded below, to which we apply the right derived functor
\mathbb{R}^{*}F
.
Then there are two spectral sequences

\displaystyle{}^{I}\!E_{2}^{p,q}=\mathbb{R}^{p}F(H^{q}(K^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}))

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{p+q}F(K^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}),

\displaystyle{}^{II}\!E_{1}^{p,q}=\mathbb{R}^{q}F(K^{p})

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{p+q}F(K^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}),

the former of which starts from the derived functor
\mathbb{R}^{*}F
applied
to the
cohomology
of the complex
K^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
, while the latter one
starts from the derived functor
\mathbb{R}^{*}F
applied to the
terms

of the complex
K^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
.
So the spectral sequence
{}^{I}\!E
expresses the point of view on

K^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
as a “deformation of its cohomology”, while the spectral
sequence
{}^{II}\!E
is an expression of the vision of
K^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
as
a “deformation of its terms”.
The condition that
K^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
is a bounded below complex ensures that
both the spectral sequences converge to the same limit (viz.,
the hypercohomology groups
\mathbb{R}^{*}F(K^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})
).

Remark 7.1
.

Let us illustrate by examples the role of convergent and “divergent”
spectral sequences in Koszul duality, as mentioned in
Section
7.2
.
We start with a convergent example; a “divergent” one will be
presented below in Remark
7.2
.

Let
A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
be a DG-algebra,
(C^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}},\gamma)
be a conilpotent
DG-coalgebra, and
\tau\colon C^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\longrightarrow A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
be twisting cochain
satisfying the equation
\tau\circ\gamma=0
(cf. Theorem
5.4
).
Let us show that the functor
M^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\longmapsto C^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\otimes^{\tau}M^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}

takes acyclic DG-modules over
A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
to acyclic DG-comodules
over
C^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
.

Indeed, let
F
be the canonical increasing filtration on
the conilpotent DG-coalgebra
C^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
(as in
Sections
3.3

and
4.3
).
Denote also by
F
the induced increasing filtration on the tensor
product
C\otimes_{k}M
; so
F_{n}(C\otimes_{k}M)=(F_{n}C)\otimes_{k}M
for all
n\geq 0
.
Then
F
is a filtration of
C^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\otimes^{\tau}M^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
by subcomplexes
(in fact, by DG-subcomodules).
Moreover, the differential on the successive quotient complexes

F_{n}(C^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\otimes^{\tau}M^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})/F_{n-1}(C^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\otimes^{\tau}M^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})
is simply
the tensor product differential on the tensor product of complexes

(F_{n}C^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}/F_{n-1}C^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})\otimes_{k}M^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
, essentially due to
the assumption that
\tau\circ\gamma=0
.
If the complex
M^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
is acyclic, it follows that the complex

F_{n}(C^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\otimes^{\tau}M^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})/F_{n-1}(C^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\otimes^{\tau}M^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})
is acyclic
as well for every
n\geq 0
.

Now the increasing filtration
F
on the complex
C^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\otimes^{\tau}M^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}

is exhaustive; so the related spectral sequence converges (this is
an easy version of
[
21
, Theorem 7.4]
), and it follows that
the complex
C^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\otimes^{\tau}M^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
is acyclic.
(Dually one can show, using a similar complete decreasing filtration,
that the functor
M^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\longmapsto\Hom^{\tau}(C^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}},M^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})
from
Sections
9.5
–
9.6
